# Daily Summary: 2025-03-07

### Developing and Utilizing a Large-Scale Cantonese Dataset for Multi-Tasking in Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.03702v1)
- **Authors**: Jiyue Jiang, Alfred Kar Yin Truong, Yanyu Chen, Qinghang Bao, Sheng Wang, Pengan Chen, Jiuming Wang, Lingpeng Kong, Yu Li, Chuan Wu
- **Abstract**: High-quality data resources play a crucial role in learning large language models (LLMs), particularly for low-resource languages like Cantonese. Despite having more than 85 million native speakers, Cantonese is still considered a low-resource language in the field of natural language processing (NLP) due to factors such as the dominance of Mandarin, lack of cohesion within the Cantonese-speaking community, diversity in character encoding and input methods, and the tendency of overseas Cantonese speakers to prefer using English. In addition, rich colloquial vocabulary of Cantonese, English loanwords, and code-switching characteristics add to the complexity of corpus collection and processing. To address these challenges, we collect Cantonese texts from a variety of sources, including open source corpora, Hong Kong-specific forums, Wikipedia, and Common Crawl data. We conduct rigorous data processing through language filtering, quality filtering, content filtering, and de-duplication steps, successfully constructing a high-quality Cantonese corpus of over 2 billion tokens for training large language models. We further refined the model through supervised fine-tuning (SFT) on curated Cantonese tasks, enhancing its ability to handle specific applications. Upon completion of the training, the model achieves state-of-the-art (SOTA) performance on four Cantonese benchmarks. After training on our dataset, the model also exhibits improved performance on other mainstream language tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the lack of high-quality Cantonese language resources for training large language models (LLMs). It describes the creation of a new Cantonese corpus called YueData, comprising over 2 billion tokens collected from diverse sources like open-source data, Hong Kong-specific forums, Wikipedia, and Common Crawl. The paper details a rigorous data processing pipeline involving language filtering, quality filtering, content filtering, and deduplication.  The authors then trained a 7b LLM, YueTung-7b, on this dataset and fine-tuned it with supervised fine-tuning (SFT) on curated Cantonese tasks. The resulting model achieved state-of-the-art (SOTA) performance on four Cantonese benchmarks and also showed improved performance on other mainstream language tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper tackles a very relevant problem: the lack of resources for low-resource languages in the NLP field. Creating a large, high-quality Cantonese dataset is undoubtedly a valuable contribution. However, the novelty and significance require a closer examination.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Resource Creation:** The main strength is the construction of YueData. Gathering and cleaning data from disparate sources is a significant engineering effort. The described data cleaning pipeline appears thorough.
    *   **Performance:** Demonstrating SOTA performance on Cantonese benchmarks provides empirical evidence of the dataset's quality and the effectiveness of the training process. The performance gains on mainstream tasks are also encouraging.
    *   **Detailed Methodology:** The paper provides a relatively detailed description of the data collection and cleaning pipeline. This is valuable for reproducibility and for others working on similar tasks for other low-resource languages.
    *   **Impact on the Cantonese NLP Community:** The paper can definitely assist the Cantonese NLP community by supplying more data, which was previously very scarce.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental Novelty:** While resource creation is valuable, the techniques used (web scraping, filtering, deduplication) are relatively standard NLP practices. The novelty lies more in the *application* of these techniques to Cantonese, rather than in fundamentally new methods.
    *   **Limited Methodological Innovation:**  The training and fine-tuning procedures used are also relatively standard.  The paper lacks methodological contributions in areas like adapting existing LLM architectures specifically for Cantonese, addressing the complexities of code-switching or dialectal variations in a more sophisticated way.
    *   **Limited Error Analysis:** While quantitative results are provided, the paper lacks a deep dive into error analysis. What types of Cantonese-specific errors does the model still make? How does it handle colloquialisms, loanwords, or code-switching in practice? Addressing this question will help further improve future models.
    *   **Bias concerns:** The model might be biased to Cantonese as spoken in Hong Kong, as this was the primary source of Cantonese data, which might limit its generalizability to other Cantonese dialects and potentially create an "us vs. them" mentality.
    *   **Lack of ablation studies**: The paper does not test different combinations of different aspects of their methodologies (e.g. data cleaning), and it is difficult to judge the significance of each component.
    *   **Dataset Availability**: The paper does not mention the availability of the YueData dataset. If the dataset is not accessible for research, its impact is significantly diminished.

*   **Significance:**

    The significance stems primarily from addressing the resource scarcity for Cantonese NLP. By providing a large, high-quality corpus, the paper facilitates further research and development in this area. However, the lack of methodological innovation limits its broader impact on the field of LLMs as a whole. The performance improvements on downstream tasks show the value of more data, which is not necessarily a new finding.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper makes a valuable contribution by creating and sharing a new resource, the lack of methodological novelty and limited exploration of error analysis prevent it from achieving a higher score. The techniques used are well-established, and the paper doesn't offer significant new insights into adapting LLMs for low-resource languages. The reliance on standard training procedures and the lack of deeper analysis of the results further lower the potential impact. The lack of publicly available data is another reason for not giving a very high score.

### A Practical Memory Injection Attack against LLM Agents
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.03704v1)
- **Authors**: Shen Dong, Shaocheng Xu, Pengfei He, Yige Li, Jiliang Tang, Tianming Liu, Hui Liu, Zhen Xiang
- **Abstract**: Agents based on large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated strong capabilities in a wide range of complex, real-world applications. However, LLM agents with a compromised memory bank may easily produce harmful outputs when the past records retrieved for demonstration are malicious. In this paper, we propose a novel Memory INJection Attack, MINJA, that enables the injection of malicious records into the memory bank by only interacting with the agent via queries and output observations. These malicious records are designed to elicit a sequence of malicious reasoning steps leading to undesirable agent actions when executing the victim user's query. Specifically, we introduce a sequence of bridging steps to link the victim query to the malicious reasoning steps. During the injection of the malicious record, we propose an indication prompt to guide the agent to autonomously generate our designed bridging steps. We also propose a progressive shortening strategy that gradually removes the indication prompt, such that the malicious record will be easily retrieved when processing the victim query comes after. Our extensive experiments across diverse agents demonstrate the effectiveness of MINJA in compromising agent memory. With minimal requirements for execution, MINJA enables any user to influence agent memory, highlighting practical risks of LLM agents.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and evaluation of the paper "A Practical Memory Injection Attack against LLM Agents":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel attack called MINJA (Memory Injection Attack) against LLM agents. Unlike prior work that assumes direct access to an agent's memory bank, MINJA demonstrates how to inject malicious records by only interacting with the agent through queries and observing outputs. The attack crafts malicious records designed to steer the agent towards undesirable actions, using bridging steps to connect victim queries to malicious reasoning, indication prompts to guide record creation, and a progressive shortening strategy to ensure efficient retrieval of the malicious records.  The authors evaluate MINJA on various agents across different tasks, showcasing its effectiveness in compromising agent memory with minimal requirements for execution.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's core novelty lies in its practical threat model. Shifting away from the assumption of direct memory manipulation is a significant and realistic advancement. Most prior work in this area requires unrealistic access. The proposed techniques – bridging steps, indication prompts, and progressive shortening – demonstrate practical innovation in crafting and injecting malicious memory records. The MINJA attack is a meaningful contribution, as it can be achieved by any user interacting with the agent and without affecting other user's queries.

*   **Significance:** The paper highlights a critical security vulnerability in LLM agents that, if unaddressed, could significantly hinder their deployment in real-world applications. If an attacker can compromise an LLM agents memory, it can influence the decision-making for other users of the same LLM agent. The experimental results, demonstrating high injection and attack success rates, are alarming and emphasize the practical risks involved.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper is well-written and clearly presents the problem, proposed solution, and experimental evaluation.
    *   The techniques are well-explained and illustrated with examples.
    *   The experimental setup is reasonably comprehensive, covering diverse agents and datasets.
    *   The results convincingly demonstrate the effectiveness of the attack.
    *   The ablation studies provide valuable insights into the different components of the attack.
    *   It emphasizes practical constraints on the attacker.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the experimental setup is good, the evaluation may benefit from being tested in real-world deployment.
    *   The paper could provide a more detailed discussion of potential defenses against the attack. It mentions that methods such as prompt engineering can be used to evade detection in LLM, but the lack of potential defense mechanism make the attack more severe.

*   **Impact:** This research will likely stimulate further work in the areas of LLM agent security, memory poisoning, and defense strategies. The focus on practical attack scenarios is crucial for ensuring the safe and reliable deployment of LLM agents in the future.

*   **Score Rationale:** While the paper doesn't introduce entirely revolutionary concepts, its practical focus and demonstrated effectiveness are significant. It meaningfully advances the field of LLM security by addressing a realistic attack vector.

### Effective LLM Knowledge Learning via Model Generalization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.03705v1)
- **Authors**: Mingkang Zhu, Xi Chen, Zhongdao Wang, Bei Yu, Hengshuang Zhao, Jiaya Jia
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) are trained on enormous documents that contain extensive world knowledge. However, it is still not well-understood how knowledge is acquired via autoregressive pre-training. This lack of understanding greatly hinders effective knowledge learning, especially for continued pretraining on up-to-date information, as this evolving information often lacks diverse repetitions like foundational knowledge. In this paper, we focus on understanding and improving LLM knowledge learning. We found and verified that knowledge learning for LLMs can be deemed as an implicit supervised task hidden in the autoregressive pre-training objective. Our findings suggest that knowledge learning for LLMs would benefit from methods designed to improve generalization ability for supervised tasks. Based on our analysis, we propose the formatting-based data augmentation to grow in-distribution samples, which does not present the risk of altering the facts embedded in documents as text paraphrasing. We also introduce sharpness-aware minimization as an effective optimization algorithm to better improve generalization. Moreover, our analysis and method can be readily extended to instruction tuning. Extensive experiment results validate our findings and demonstrate our methods' effectiveness in both continued pre-training and instruction tuning. This paper offers new perspectives and insights to interpret and design effective strategies for LLM knowledge learning.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the mechanisms of knowledge acquisition in Large Language Models (LLMs) during autoregressive pre-training. It proposes that knowledge learning in LLMs can be viewed as an implicit supervised task. Based on this, the authors suggest that improving the generalization capabilities of LLMs, through techniques like formatting-based data augmentation (which doesn't alter factual content) and sharpness-aware minimization (SAM), can enhance knowledge learning. They extend their methods to instruction tuning, showing improved performance in both continued pre-training and instruction tuning phases, particularly when generalizing across diverse question formulations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of framing knowledge learning in LLMs as a supervised task hidden within autoregressive pre-training is a valuable insight. While previous works have looked at knowledge retrieval and frequency correlations, this paper offers a more systematic perspective. The specific implementation of formatting-based data augmentation is reasonably novel and addresses a key concern of maintaining factual accuracy while increasing in-distribution samples. SAM has been used in other contexts, but its application to LLM knowledge learning within the proposed framework adds to the novelty. The approach to diverse question formulation during instruction tuning, although mentioned briefly, is also a noteworthy point as many prior studies concentrate primarily on varying topics/domains rather than question phrasing.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a very important and timely problem: improving the efficiency and effectiveness of knowledge updates in LLMs. Given the continuous evolution of information, the ability to rapidly incorporate new knowledge without relying on massive datasets with diverse repetitions is crucial. The methods proposed have the potential to make continued pre-training more practical and efficient. The validation on synthesized biographical data and the Wiki2023-film datasets lends credibility to the findings.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper clearly articulates the problem and provides a coherent theoretical framework.
    *   The experimental results, particularly the gains in EM, Recall, and F1 scores compared to baselines, support the claims.
    *   The ablation studies provide insight into the contribution of each component of the proposed method.
    *   The formatting-based data augmentation is a practical and resource-efficient technique.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The paper relies heavily on synthetic or regenerated data, which might not fully reflect the complexities of real-world knowledge learning. While Wiki2023-film data is used to mitigate this concern, the source and generation approach is exactly same as reported by the authors. The model must also be pretrained on Wiki or similar datasets, which may affect the effectiveness of the proposed method.
    *   The instruction tuning component, while promising, could be further developed with more in-depth analysis and experimentation on diverse datasets.
    *   The theoretical framework lacks a deep mathematical underpinning. While the analogy to supervised learning is helpful, a more rigorous mathematical justification would strengthen the paper.
    *   The paper offers a limited analysis of the computational cost associated with the proposed methods (SAM and formatting).

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in LLM knowledge learning by providing a new perspective and practical techniques for improvement. The idea of viewing knowledge learning as an implicit supervised task could inspire further theoretical and empirical investigations. The formatting-based data augmentation and SAM offer a cost-effective approach to knowledge updates.
* **Rigorous Rationale:** The paper presents a reasonably strong case for its claims through experiments and analysis. But, the dependence on synthetic and rebuilt data and an overall lack of a theoretical foundation weakens the paper.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:** The paper offers a novel perspective on LLM knowledge learning, supported by experimental evidence. Its findings contribute valuable insights and practical techniques for efficient knowledge updating. However, the limitations regarding the datasets used and the lack of theoretical rigor prevent it from achieving a higher score. The paper is a solid contribution, but further research is needed to address its weaknesses and fully realize its potential impact.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Rethinking Video Tokenization: A Conditioned Diffusion-based Approach
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.03708v1)
- **Authors**: Nianzu Yang, Pandeng Li, Liming Zhao, Yang Li, Chen-Wei Xie, Yehui Tang, Xudong Lu, Zhihang Liu, Yun Zheng, Yu Liu, Junchi Yan
- **Abstract**: Video tokenizers, which transform videos into compact latent representations, are key to video generation. Existing video tokenizers are based on the VAE architecture and follow a paradigm where an encoder compresses videos into compact latents, and a deterministic decoder reconstructs the original videos from these latents. In this paper, we propose a novel \underline{\textbf{C}}onditioned \underline{\textbf{D}}iffusion-based video \underline{\textbf{T}}okenizer entitled \textbf{\ourmethod}, which departs from previous methods by replacing the deterministic decoder with a 3D causal diffusion model. The reverse diffusion generative process of the decoder is conditioned on the latent representations derived via the encoder. With a feature caching and sampling acceleration, the framework efficiently reconstructs high-fidelity videos of arbitrary lengths. Results show that {\ourmethod} achieves state-of-the-art performance in video reconstruction tasks using just a single-step sampling. Even a smaller version of {\ourmethod} still achieves reconstruction results on par with the top two baselines. Furthermore, the latent video generation model trained using {\ourmethod} also shows superior performance.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel Conditioned Diffusion-based video Tokenizer (CDT) for compressing videos into compact latent representations, a crucial step for efficient video generation.  Unlike existing VAE-based tokenizers that use deterministic decoders, CDT replaces the decoder with a 3D causal diffusion model conditioned on the encoded latent representations. This approach enables high-fidelity video reconstruction of arbitrary lengths through a reverse diffusion generative process.  The framework incorporates feature caching and sampling acceleration (DDIM) to enhance efficiency.  Experiments demonstrate state-of-the-art video reconstruction performance with even a smaller version of CDT achieving results comparable to top baselines. The latent space learned using CDT also shows superior video generation performance when used within the Latte framework.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in replacing the deterministic decoder in a video tokenizer with a conditioned diffusion model. While diffusion models are already used in video generation, applying them *within* a tokenizer framework, *replacing* the standard deterministic reconstruction is a significant change. The use of a 3D causal diffusion model is reasonable and appropriate for the video domain, ensuring temporal consistency. However, 3D causal convolutions and UNet architecture are relatively standard components; the core novelty lies in the decoder replacement itself. Feature caching for long videos and DDIM acceleration are well-established techniques, their integration, while useful, does not constitute groundbreaking innovation.

*   **Significance:** The significance lies in demonstrating that a diffusion-based decoder can not only match but potentially surpass the reconstruction quality of deterministic decoders, leading to better latent spaces for downstream video generation. The efficiency gains, particularly through DDIM, are essential for practical application. Showing that a *smaller* model (CDT-S) can compete with larger baselines suggests an architectural advantage or more efficient training. The superior performance in video generation (lower FVD) using CDT’s latent space further reinforces its potential impact.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   Well-structured methodology explanation.
    *   Comprehensive experimental evaluation comparing to strong baselines.
    *   Ablation studies to analyze the impact of different components.
    *   Demonstration of practical efficiency through optimized sampling.
    *   Superior performance in latent video generation is particularly promising.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the paper achieves efficiency, it would be beneficial to compare against more recent and efficient tokenizers.
    *   The novelty of feature caching and DDIM is limited, as they're already well-known techniques. A more in-depth analysis of why they perform well in the CDT context would add value.
    *   Lack of ablation study of causal convolution.
    *   The improvement in reconstruction does not seem as significant as in video generation.
    *   Latte is relatively old video generation model and is not as state-of-the-art.
    *   The experiments are primarily focused on video reconstruction and generation and do not consider other use-cases, such as video editing or anomaly detection, which would be a better demonstration.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the design of future video tokenizers, prompting researchers to explore diffusion-based alternatives to deterministic decoders.  The improved latent spaces could lead to better downstream video generation models. It demonstrates the feasibility of integrating diffusion models into this specific component of the video generation pipeline, potentially opening new avenues for research.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a significant, but not revolutionary, contribution. While the core idea of replacing the deterministic decoder with a conditioned diffusion model is novel and impactful, the other components (feature caching, DDIM) are relatively standard. The empirical results are convincing, showing state-of-the-art reconstruction performance and superior video generation. However, more recent video tokenizers were missing for fair comparisons.

### Improving LLM Safety Alignment with Dual-Objective Optimization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.03710v1)
- **Authors**: Xuandong Zhao, Will Cai, Tianneng Shi, David Huang, Licong Lin, Song Mei, Dawn Song
- **Abstract**: Existing training-time safety alignment techniques for large language models (LLMs) remain vulnerable to jailbreak attacks. Direct preference optimization (DPO), a widely deployed alignment method, exhibits limitations in both experimental and theoretical contexts as its loss function proves suboptimal for refusal learning. Through gradient-based analysis, we identify these shortcomings and propose an improved safety alignment that disentangles DPO objectives into two components: (1) robust refusal training, which encourages refusal even when partial unsafe generations are produced, and (2) targeted unlearning of harmful knowledge. This approach significantly increases LLM robustness against a wide range of jailbreak attacks, including prefilling, suffix, and multi-turn attacks across both in-distribution and out-of-distribution scenarios. Furthermore, we introduce a method to emphasize critical refusal tokens by incorporating a reward-based token-level weighting mechanism for refusal learning, which further improves the robustness against adversarial exploits. Our research also suggests that robustness to jailbreak attacks is correlated with token distribution shifts in the training process and internal representations of refusal and harmful tokens, offering valuable directions for future research in LLM safety alignment. The code is available at https://github.com/wicai24/DOOR-Alignment
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Improving LLM Safety Alignment with Dual-Objective Optimization" addresses the vulnerability of large language models (LLMs) to jailbreak attacks, even after alignment with Direct Preference Optimization (DPO). It argues that DPO's loss function is suboptimal for refusal learning, leading to insufficient robustness against adversarial prompts. The paper identifies two key limitations of DPO: (1) an imbalance in learning rate, where harmful responses are suppressed more readily than safe responses are reinforced, and (2) poor generalization to out-of-distribution (OOD) safety scenarios.

To overcome these shortcomings, the authors introduce Dual-Objective Optimization for Refusal (DOOR). DOOR comprises two components: (1) *robust refusal training*, which uses data augmentation (prepending harmful content to prompts) to encourage refusal even with partial unsafe generations, and (2) *targeted unlearning* using Negative Preference Optimization (NPO) to actively penalize harmful knowledge.  They further enhance this approach with Weighted DOOR (W-DOOR), which uses a reward-based token-level weighting mechanism to emphasize critical refusal tokens.

The paper presents empirical evaluations showing that DOOR and W-DOOR significantly improve LLM robustness against various jailbreak attacks (prefilling, suffix, multi-turn) on both in-distribution and OOD data. The results also indicate better preservation of general capabilities compared to DPO. The authors analyze the gradient dynamics of DOOR and W-DOOR, and they examine token distribution shifts and internal representations to understand the effectiveness of their approach.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in the combination of techniques within the DOOR framework. While data augmentation and NPO have been explored previously, their integration to address specific weaknesses of DPO in safety alignment is novel. The token-level weighting in W-DOOR is a further refinement that differentiates the approach. The analysis of gradient dynamics and token distributions provides valuable insights.

*   **Significance:** The problem of jailbreak attacks against aligned LLMs is highly significant, as it directly impacts the safety and reliability of these models. The paper provides a practical solution that demonstrates improved robustness against a range of attacks.  The identification of DPO's limitations in refusal learning is also a valuable contribution.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear problem definition:** The paper clearly identifies the vulnerabilities of DPO in safety alignment.
    *   **Well-motivated approach:** The proposed DOOR framework is logically derived from the analysis of DPO's limitations.
    *   **Strong empirical results:** The experiments demonstrate a consistent improvement in robustness across various attack types and datasets.
    *   **In-depth analysis:**  The gradient analysis, token distribution studies, and reward visualizations provide a deeper understanding of the mechanisms at play.
    *   **Open-source code:** The release of the code promotes reproducibility and facilitates further research.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited model scope:** The evaluation focuses on Gemma and Llama models. While these are popular, it would be valuable to see how DOOR generalizes to other architectures.
    *   **Parameter sensitivity:** The paper mentions the sensitivity of token-level weighting parameters. A more systematic study of the optimal settings and their impact on performance would be beneficial.
    *   **Generalization of Simulation Method for Harmful Responses:** The paper hinges on a simulation process for harmful responses, relying on fine-tuning with previously existing malicious datasets. This opens up the question of whether those datasets are sufficiently diverse and capture the breadth of possible attacks, or if the results overfit the set of attacks the "jailbroken" model is trained to output.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of safer LLMs by providing a more effective training methodology for refusal learning. The analysis of DPO's limitations and the insights into token-level safety refinements offer valuable guidance for future research in this area.

*   **Overall:** The paper presents a well-executed study with strong empirical evidence and insightful analysis. The DOOR framework addresses a critical problem in LLM safety and offers a practical solution with good generalization capabilities. While further research is needed to explore the parameter sensitivity and generalization to other models, the paper represents a significant contribution to the field.

### Towards Understanding Distilled Reasoning Models: A Representational Approach
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.03730v1)
- **Authors**: David D. Baek, Max Tegmark
- **Abstract**: In this paper, we investigate how model distillation impacts the development of reasoning features in large language models (LLMs). To explore this, we train a crosscoder on Qwen-series models and their fine-tuned variants. Our results suggest that the crosscoder learns features corresponding to various types of reasoning, including self-reflection and computation verification. Moreover, we observe that distilled models contain unique reasoning feature directions, which could be used to steer the model into over-thinking or incisive-thinking mode. In particular, we perform analysis on four specific reasoning categories: (a) self-reflection, (b) deductive reasoning, (c) alternative reasoning, and (d) contrastive reasoning. Finally, we examine the changes in feature geometry resulting from the distillation process and find indications that larger distilled models may develop more structured representations, which correlate with enhanced distillation performance. By providing insights into how distillation modifies the model, our study contributes to enhancing the transparency and reliability of AI systems.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Towards Understanding Distilled Reasoning Models: A Representational Approach":

**Summary:**

The paper investigates how model distillation affects the reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs). It trains a sparse crosscoder on Qwen-series models (base and fine-tuned variants) to identify and analyze reasoning features. The authors find that distilled models develop unique reasoning features, including self-reflection and computation verification. They explore feature geometry changes resulting from distillation, finding that larger distilled models show indications of more structured representations correlating with improved distillation performance. They also demonstrate the ability to steer the model using these reasoning features, encouraging over-thinking or incisive-thinking behaviors.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a valuable investigation into the *representational changes* induced by model distillation, a critical area that has received relatively less attention than the *performance* aspects of distillation. The use of a sparse crosscoder to identify unique reasoning features is a reasonable and effective approach. The finding that distilled models develop distinct reasoning features, especially self-reflection and verification, is a novel contribution. The demonstration of steering a model into different reasoning modes using these features is also a significant finding. While previous research examined model distillation effects empirically, this paper presents a novel approach to understanding those effects through feature analysis and representational geometry.
*   **Significance:** The research addresses a key gap in understanding *how* distillation impacts model behavior. The paper offers insights into improving the transparency and reliability of AI systems, as understanding the internal mechanisms of these models is crucial.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Research Questions:** The paper starts with well-defined and relevant research questions.
    *   **Methodological Rigor:** The use of sparse crosscoders, feature ablation experiments, and steering techniques provides a solid methodological basis for their conclusions.
    *   **Empirical Evidence:** The paper provides strong empirical evidence to support its claims, with clear visualizations and statistical analysis.
    *   **Reproducibility:** The paper provides sufficient details regarding data, models, and training processes, so the experiment is in principle reproducible.
    *   **Practical Implications:** The potential for steering models into specific reasoning modes opens avenues for controlling and tailoring LLM behavior.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Scope:** The study focuses solely on Qwen-series models. While a reasonable starting point, the findings may not generalize to other architectures or pretraining regimes.
    *   **Small Scale of Analysis:** Although Qwen models are analyzed, the number of examples used to demonstrate steering and analyze feature causality could be considered limited.
    *   **Interpretation Challenges:** Defining reasoning categories (self-reflection, deductive, etc.) can be somewhat subjective, potentially influencing the feature selection and analysis process. While the authors attempt to ground these in specific tokens, the interpretation remains a potential source of bias.
    *   **Limited quantitative evaluation on the steering experiment:** Showing a few qualitative examples of how the steering changes the behavior is not enough, ideally some metric should be devised to quantitatively measure the success of steering.
    *   **Parallelogram Loss:** While the parallelogram loss analysis offers insights into feature geometry, it's only one measure of representational structure. Other metrics could be considered to provide a more comprehensive view.
*   **Potential Influence:** The paper's approach and findings have the potential to influence future research on model interpretability, distillation techniques, and the development of more controllable AI systems. By providing a methodology for analyzing representational changes in distilled models, it can guide future studies in this area. The insights into feature steering could lead to practical methods for customizing LLM behavior.
*   **Score Justification:** The paper makes a significant contribution to the field of LLM interpretability, specifically in understanding the effects of model distillation. The approach is novel, the methodology is rigorous, and the findings have practical implications. However, the limited scope and potential challenges in interpreting reasoning categories and the limitation of evaluation hold it back from a higher score.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### RiskAgent: Autonomous Medical AI Copilot for Generalist Risk Prediction
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.03802v1)
- **Authors**: Fenglin Liu, Jinge Wu, Hongjian Zhou, Xiao Gu, Soheila Molaei, Anshul Thakur, Lei Clifton, Honghan Wu, David A. Clifton
- **Abstract**: The application of Large Language Models (LLMs) to various clinical applications has attracted growing research attention. However, real-world clinical decision-making differs significantly from the standardized, exam-style scenarios commonly used in current efforts. In this paper, we present the RiskAgent system to perform a broad range of medical risk predictions, covering over 387 risk scenarios across diverse complex diseases, e.g., cardiovascular disease and cancer. RiskAgent is designed to collaborate with hundreds of clinical decision tools, i.e., risk calculators and scoring systems that are supported by evidence-based medicine. To evaluate our method, we have built the first benchmark MedRisk specialized for risk prediction, including 12,352 questions spanning 154 diseases, 86 symptoms, 50 specialties, and 24 organ systems. The results show that our RiskAgent, with 8 billion model parameters, achieves 76.33% accuracy, outperforming the most recent commercial LLMs, o1, o3-mini, and GPT-4.5, and doubling the 38.39% accuracy of GPT-4o. On rare diseases, e.g., Idiopathic Pulmonary Fibrosis (IPF), RiskAgent outperforms o1 and GPT-4.5 by 27.27% and 45.46% accuracy, respectively. Finally, we further conduct a generalization evaluation on an external evidence-based diagnosis benchmark and show that our RiskAgent achieves the best results. These encouraging results demonstrate the great potential of our solution for diverse diagnosis domains. To improve the adaptability of our model in different scenarios, we have built and open-sourced a family of models ranging from 1 billion to 70 billion parameters. Our code, data, and models are all available at https://github.com/AI-in-Health/RiskAgent.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "RiskAgent: Autonomous Medical AI Copilot for Generalist Risk Prediction":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces RiskAgent, an AI system designed to perform generalist medical risk prediction across a broad range of diseases and scenarios.  RiskAgent leverages a modular architecture composed of three LLM-powered agents (Decider, Executor, and Reviewer) that collaborate with existing evidence-based medical tools, such as risk calculators and scoring systems.  The Decider selects relevant tools, the Executor parses parameters and executes them, and the Reviewer analyzes the process for correctness and provides feedback.  The authors create a new benchmark dataset, MedRisk, to evaluate the system, and demonstrate that RiskAgent (an 8B parameter model) outperforms state-of-the-art general and medical LLMs, including GPT-4.5 and Meditron-70B, in terms of prediction accuracy.  The paper further demonstrates the system's adaptability and generalizability through external validation and ablation studies, highlighting the benefits of leveraging existing medical knowledge and a collaborative agent-based approach.  The code, data, and models are made publicly available.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the architecture of RiskAgent, which explicitly combines LLMs with existing, validated medical risk assessment tools. This is a departure from simply fine-tuning LLMs on medical data, which can be data-hungry and prone to hallucinations.  The modular approach, with distinct roles for the Decider, Executor, and Reviewer, is also a novel contribution that facilitates collaboration and enables more transparent decision-making. Building a dedicated benchmark, MedRisk, for risk prediction is also a valuable contribution, as existing medical benchmarks often focus on question answering or other tasks.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial gap in the application of LLMs to real-world clinical decision-making.  By focusing on risk prediction and leveraging existing medical tools, the authors create a system that is both more accurate and more trustworthy than existing LLM-based approaches.  The improvements in accuracy are significant, particularly in complex and rare diseases. By making the code and data publicly available, the authors are encouraging further research and development in this area. Additionally, it is stated in the paper that the model is privacy friendly due to its small parameter size. This could make it more easily applicable in practice, as fewer patient privacy rules may need to be applied to its deployment. This is therefore a great step forward in building AI tools for resource limited clinical applications.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Strong empirical results demonstrating improved accuracy compared to state-of-the-art LLMs.
    *   Novel architecture that combines LLMs with existing medical tools.
    *   Creation of a new benchmark dataset, MedRisk.
    *   Detailed ablation studies showing the contribution of each component.
    *   Good evidence for generalizability across different LLMs.
    *   Publicly available code, data, and models.
    *   Privacy-friendly, 8 billion parameter model.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The benchmark dataset is synthesized using LLMs (GPT-4o), which introduces potential biases. While manual filtering was performed, biases may persist.
    *   The reliance on MDCalc limits the scope of the tool library.  MDCalc is comprehensive, but there are other valuable risk assessment tools available.
    *   The "black-box" nature of the tool outputs: Although the system enhances reasoning and integration, the underlying calculation of those tools remains opaque. This also leads to the tool being only as good as the underlying medical tools themselves. For example, it relies on the tools being robust and accurate, which could lead to problems if the tools themselves are outdated.
    *   It's unclear how the prompt engineering was optimized. More details on the prompt design process would strengthen the paper.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence the development of AI systems for medical decision-making. The collaborative, agent-based approach could be adopted by other researchers and developers. The MedRisk benchmark will likely become a standard for evaluating risk prediction systems.  The focus on trustworthiness and explainability is also important, and could help to increase the adoption of AI in clinical settings.

**Score:** 7/10

**Justification:**

The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of medical AI. While the use of LLMs in healthcare is a growing area, RiskAgent's approach of leveraging existing medical knowledge, combined with a modular agent-based architecture, is a notable advancement. The creation of the MedRisk benchmark fills a gap and enables more rigorous evaluation of risk prediction systems. The strong empirical results and the public availability of the resources are also positive aspects.

However, the limitations of the benchmark dataset (potential bias from GPT-4o generation) and the reliance on MDCalc somewhat temper the overall impact. More details are needed on how to optimize the LLM agents. The value of the contribution would also be significantly higher if the "black-box" tools could be made more transparent. As such, the paper falls short of being truly transformative, and therefore merits a high, but not top-tier, score.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Vision-Language Models Struggle to Align Entities across Modalities
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.03854v1)
- **Authors**: Iñigo Alonso, Ander Salaberria, Gorka Azkune, Jeremy Barnes, Oier Lopez de Lacalle
- **Abstract**: Cross-modal entity linking refers to the ability to align entities and their attributes across different modalities. While cross-modal entity linking is a fundamental skill needed for real-world applications such as multimodal code generation, fake news detection, or scene understanding, it has not been thoroughly studied in the literature. In this paper, we introduce a new task and benchmark to address this gap. Our benchmark, MATE, consists of 5.5k evaluation instances featuring visual scenes aligned with their textual representations. To evaluate cross-modal entity linking performance, we design a question-answering task that involves retrieving one attribute of an object in one modality based on a unique attribute of that object in another modality. We evaluate state-of-the-art Vision-Language Models (VLMs) and humans on this task, and find that VLMs struggle significantly compared to humans, particularly as the number of objects in the scene increases. Our analysis also shows that, while chain-of-thought prompting can improve VLM performance, models remain far from achieving human-level proficiency. These findings highlight the need for further research in cross-modal entity linking and show that MATE is a strong benchmark to support that progress.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a new benchmark, MATE, designed to evaluate the cross-modal entity linking abilities of Vision-Language Models (VLMs). Cross-modal entity linking refers to the ability to align entities and their attributes across different modalities (visual and textual in this case).  The benchmark consists of synthetic images of 3D scenes paired with textual representations (JSON format). A question-answering task is designed where models must retrieve an attribute of an object in one modality based on a unique attribute of that object in the other modality. The authors evaluate several state-of-the-art VLMs and compare their performance to human baselines. The results show that VLMs significantly underperform compared to humans, particularly as the scene complexity (number of objects) increases.  The authors analyze the failure modes and find that the cross-modal linking is the key bottleneck, rather than difficulties in processing information within a single modality. They also explore the effect of chain-of-thought prompting, which provides limited improvement.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in the introduction of the MATE benchmark.  While cross-modal understanding has been explored, the specific task of *explicitly* evaluating entity linking across modalities using a controlled, synthetic dataset is relatively novel. Prior work has often focused on tasks like image captioning or visual question answering, where entity linking is implicit.  The synthetic nature allows for better control and analysis of different factors that affect the model's performance (e.g., number of objects, attribute types).  The focus on *linking attributes* as opposed to whole object grounding is also a distinct and novel contribution.  Existing benchmarks like CLEVR, which MATE is based on, focus more on reasoning about relationships between objects.

**Significance:** The significance of this work stems from several aspects:

*   **Highlighting a Gap:**  It clearly demonstrates a weakness in current VLMs: the difficulty in performing accurate cross-modal entity linking. This is a fundamental skill for many real-world applications.
*   **Controlled Evaluation:** MATE provides a controlled environment to study this weakness, enabling researchers to diagnose the problems and develop more targeted solutions. The careful design of the benchmark with balanced distributions and consideration of factors such as object count and attribute types enhances its utility.
*   **Analysis of Failure Modes:** The analysis of where VLMs struggle (e.g., more complex scenes, specific attribute types) provides valuable insights for future research directions. It shows that unimodal capabilities are strong, pinpointing that the bottleneck lies in the linking process.
*   **Human Baseline:**  Comparing VLM performance to human performance provides a clear target and highlights the substantial gap that still exists.
*   **Practical Implications:** The analysis reveals that as the number of objects in the scene increases, there is significant performance degradation, thus implying real-world applicability where scenes are complex are where the VLMs struggle.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Synthetic Data:** The use of synthetic data is both a strength and a weakness. While it allows for controlled experiments, it raises questions about the generalizability of the findings to real-world images, which are far more complex and diverse.
*   **Limited Modalities:** The benchmark focuses solely on visual and textual modalities.  Extending it to other modalities (e.g., audio, haptic) would broaden its applicability.
*   **Task Simplicity:** The question-answering task, while effective for evaluation, is relatively simple. More complex reasoning tasks involving the linked entities could provide a more comprehensive assessment.
*   **Scope of Models:** While multiple models were tested, testing on an even more extensive range of architectures and sizes would add further robustness to the claims.
*   **Lack of Solutions:** The paper primarily diagnoses the problem but doesn't propose or evaluate specific solutions to improve cross-modal entity linking.

**Justification for Score:**

I assign a score of **7**.

*   The paper makes a solid contribution by identifying and characterizing a specific weakness in current VLMs (cross-modal entity linking).
*   The MATE benchmark is a well-designed resource that enables controlled and targeted evaluation. The decision to focus on linking *attributes* is an interesting contribution.
*   The analysis of failure modes provides valuable insights.
*   However, the reliance on synthetic data limits the immediate applicability of the findings.
*   More importantly, the paper diagnoses the problem but offers little in the way of solutions or directions for addressing the challenges. The CoT experiments offered a little improvement. This is often a symptom of good problem definition but leaves further studies for future work.

In summary, the paper is valuable for its problem definition, careful analysis, and the introduction of a useful benchmark, but it could be strengthened by addressing some of the limitations mentioned above, particularly concerning real-world applicability and exploring potential solutions. The score reflects the impact of the work relative to the limitations.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### LEWIS (LayEr WIse Sparsity) -- A Training Free Guided Model Merging Approach
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.03874v1)
- **Authors**: Hetarth Chopra, Vidhi Rambhia, Vikram Adve
- **Abstract**: As specialized large language models (LLMs) become increasingly prevalent, model merging methods are being used to combine them to create a single multi-task model without requiring any additional data or training. However, these approaches fall short when the objective of merging is to increase the downstream model's performance on a particular task-specific benchmark. In this work, we propose LEWIS (Layer Wise Sparsity), a guided model-merging framework that uses activation-based layer importance to dynamically adjust layer-wise task-vector sparsity required for the merge process. LEWIS uses a calibration dataset to prioritize critical layers during the task-vector pruning process required for model merging. This approach guides existing merging methods by preserving essential layer-wise task-specific knowledge while ensuring the merged model performs the best at benchmarks resembling the calibration dataset. Our experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of LEWIS with performance improvements of code instruction-following and math-solving models created through model merging up to 4 percent and 11.3 percent, respectively, outperforming unguided data-less model merging approaches that use uniform-sparsity.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces LEWIS (Layer Wise Sparsity), a training-free, guided model merging approach. LEWIS leverages a calibration dataset to determine layer importance based on activation norms and then uses this information to guide task-vector pruning during model merging. This approach dynamically adjusts the layer-wise task-vector sparsity, preserving critical task-specific knowledge in important layers. Experiments demonstrate performance improvements on code instruction-following and math-solving models compared to unguided model merging approaches that use uniform sparsity.  Specifically, LEWIS is used to improve TIES and DARE model merging strategies.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's core novelty lies in its use of a *calibration dataset* combined with activation norm differences between the fine-tuned and pre-trained models to guide the sparsity structure of the merged model. This is a useful addition to existing model merging techniques, like TIES and DARE, which typically employ uniform or magnitude-based pruning of task vectors. The idea of incorporating layer-wise importance based on activation differences is reasonable and intuitively sound. It connects insights from pruning literature (like Wanda) to the model merging domain.

*   **Significance:** The significance is moderate. The reported performance improvements, while present (up to 4% on code and 11.3% on math), are not groundbreaking. Also, the improvements appear to be very specific to benchmark datasets resembling the calibration data, which limits the potential of this method on other datasets. The results show the method's effectiveness within specific task domains, which has practical value. The application of layer-wise importance as a tool for boosting existing methods provides an incremental advancement in the realm of data-less model merging.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The approach is training-free, which is valuable in resource-constrained scenarios.
    *   The method is general and can be combined with different model merging algorithms (TIES, DARE).
    *   The empirical results demonstrate improvements, particularly on the math task.
    *   The paper explores ablation studies about top-k layers.
    *   The description of the algorithm and experimental setup is clear and well-organized.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The performance improvements, while present, are not substantial.  The gains are also sensitive to the selection of sparsity bounds [γ, ε], which necessitates experimentation.
    *   The approach relies on a calibration dataset, which may not always be readily available or representative of all target tasks. This reduces the "data-less" aspect somewhat, and the choice of this dataset may bias towards the calibration datasets.
    *   The experimental evaluation is limited to a few datasets and model architectures.  Generalizability to other tasks and models needs further investigation.
    *   The paper provides little explanation as to how the clipping bounds are empirically tested. More experimental information about the clipping bounds might be necessary.
    *   The ablation studies, while interesting, need more depth. Showing the results for only Gemma-2b-it is a significant limitation.
    *   Results are not consistently better than baselines and for certain experiments, the baseline outperforms the algorithm.
    *   The paper only includes the merging of two models. Further studies on merging more models should be performed.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper contributes to the ongoing research on efficient model merging techniques. The idea of using calibration data to guide layer-wise sparsity could inspire further research into more sophisticated ways of incorporating task-specific knowledge into the merging process. It may lead to new approaches that combine data-driven and data-less methods for model merging.

**Score: 6**

**Rationale:** The paper presents a novel and reasonable approach to model merging. The experimental results provide some evidence of its effectiveness, however, the gains are not substantial, and the method has significant limitations regarding the need for a calibration dataset and sensitivity to hyperparameter tuning. The potential influence is moderate as it proposes an incremental refinement to existing model merging strategies rather than a disruptive breakthrough. Future research is required to address the identified weaknesses and explore its generalizability.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### Pretrained LLMs as Real-Time Controllers for Robot Operated Serial Production Line
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.03889v1)
- **Authors**: Muhammad Waseem, Kshitij Bhatta, Chen Li, Qing Chang
- **Abstract**: The manufacturing industry is undergoing a transformative shift, driven by cutting-edge technologies like 5G, AI, and cloud computing. Despite these advancements, effective system control, which is crucial for optimizing production efficiency, remains a complex challenge due to the intricate, knowledge-dependent nature of manufacturing processes and the reliance on domain-specific expertise. Conventional control methods often demand heavy customization, considerable computational resources, and lack transparency in decision-making. In this work, we investigate the feasibility of using Large Language Models (LLMs), particularly GPT-4, as a straightforward, adaptable solution for controlling manufacturing systems, specifically, mobile robot scheduling. We introduce an LLM-based control framework to assign mobile robots to different machines in robot assisted serial production lines, evaluating its performance in terms of system throughput. Our proposed framework outperforms traditional scheduling approaches such as First-Come-First-Served (FCFS), Shortest Processing Time (SPT), and Longest Processing Time (LPT). While it achieves performance that is on par with state-of-the-art methods like Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning (MARL), it offers a distinct advantage by delivering comparable throughput without the need for extensive retraining. These results suggest that the proposed LLM-based solution is well-suited for scenarios where technical expertise, computational resources, and financial investment are limited, while decision transparency and system scalability are critical concerns.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the potential of using Large Language Models (LLMs), specifically GPT-4, as real-time controllers for robot-operated serial production lines.  The authors propose an LLM-based control framework to schedule mobile robots in assigning them to different machines. The framework is compared with traditional scheduling approaches (FCFS, SPT, LPT) and a Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning (MARL) approach in terms of system throughput. Results indicate that the LLM-based approach outperforms the traditional methods and achieves comparable performance to MARL, but without requiring extensive retraining. The authors highlight the advantages of LLMs in scenarios with limited technical expertise, computational resources, and financial investment, where decision transparency and system scalability are critical.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the application of pre-trained LLMs to real-time control, particularly in the context of robot scheduling within manufacturing. While LLMs have seen increasing use in manufacturing, their application to dynamic, real-time control problems is relatively unexplored. The explicit structuring of prompts and the definition of feasibility criteria to address the LLM's inherent limitations (hallucinations) are also noteworthy. However, the core idea of using LLMs for control, while novel in this *specific* context, builds upon the broader trend of leveraging LLMs in various automation tasks.

* **Significance:** The significance stems from the potential to democratize advanced control methods. The paper argues that LLMs can provide a viable alternative to resource-intensive RL/DRL approaches, particularly for small and medium-sized enterprises. The explainability aspect, enabling users to understand and trust the system's decisions, is a significant advantage. The adaptability of LLMs to system changes without retraining also addresses a major bottleneck in traditional control systems. The reduction in the need of extensive hyperparameter tuning, and specialised knowledge is also a significant contribution of LLMs.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly defines the problem and articulates the limitations of existing control methods.
    * **Well-Structured Framework:** The proposed LLM-based control framework is well-defined, with a clear explanation of its components and functionalities.
    * **Comparative Analysis:** The comparison with established methods (FCFS, SPT, LPT, MARL) provides a strong basis for evaluating the LLM approach.
    * **Emphasis on Explainability:** The focus on providing interpretable decisions is a key strength, addressing a critical issue in many AI-driven systems.
    * **Practical Implications:** The paper highlights the potential for practical applications in real-world manufacturing scenarios.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Empirical Validation:** The experiments, while present, are based on *synthetic* data. While the authors justify this, validation on real-world manufacturing systems is crucial to fully assess the framework's robustness and effectiveness.  The reliance on a *single* episode length and a relatively small number of repetitions (25) could also limit the generality of the conclusions.
    * **Performance Gap with MARL:** While the LLM approach performs competitively, it doesn't outperform MARL.  The paper attributes this to the lack of environment-specific training in the LLM. Further investigation into bridging this performance gap could strengthen the findings. A more thorough analysis of the trade-offs between performance and the other benefits of LLMs (explainability, adaptability) would also be valuable.
    * **Hallucination Mitigation:** While the paper mentions the mitigation of hallucinations using feasibility criteria, a more detailed discussion and empirical evaluation of this aspect would be beneficial.  Quantifying the reduction in hallucination rates would strengthen the argument. The impact of the type of prompt, as well as length of the prompt is not investigated.
    * **Scalability beyond prompts**: The paper mentions that prompt changes can be made to change the system, this however may prove to be difficult as prompts increase in size. A more in depth analysis of this would be beneficial.

* **Potential Influence:**  The paper has the potential to stimulate further research into the use of LLMs for real-time control. It could influence the development of more accessible and adaptable control systems for manufacturing and other domains. The emphasis on explainability could also encourage the adoption of more transparent AI solutions.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel application of LLMs with practical implications. The framework is well-structured, and the comparative analysis demonstrates its potential. However, the reliance on synthetic data, the performance gap with MARL, and the limited discussion on hallucination mitigation temper the overall assessment. The paper would be significantly stronger with more extensive real-world validation and a more in-depth analysis of the identified limitations.

Score: 6.5

- **Classification**: cs.RO
- **Score**: 6/10

### On the Convergence of Adam-Type Algorithm for Bilevel Optimization under Unbounded Smoothness
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.03908v1)
- **Authors**: Xiaochuan Gong, Jie Hao, Mingrui Liu
- **Abstract**: Adam has become one of the most popular optimizers for training modern deep neural networks, such as transformers. However, its applicability is largely restricted to single-level optimization problems. In this paper, we aim to extend vanilla Adam to tackle bilevel optimization problems, which have important applications in machine learning, such as meta-learning. In particular, we study stochastic bilevel optimization problems where the lower-level function is strongly convex and the upper-level objective is nonconvex with potentially unbounded smoothness. This unbounded smooth objective function covers a broad class of neural networks, including transformers, which may exhibit non-Lipschitz gradients. In this work, we introduce AdamBO, a single-loop Adam-type method that achieves $\widetilde{O}(\epsilon^{-4})$ oracle complexity to find $\epsilon$-stationary points, where the oracle calls involve stochastic gradient or Hessian/Jacobian-vector product evaluations. The key to our analysis is a novel randomness decoupling lemma that provides refined control over the lower-level variable. We conduct extensive experiments on various machine learning tasks involving bilevel formulations with recurrent neural networks (RNNs) and transformers, demonstrating the effectiveness of our proposed Adam-type algorithm.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper tackles bilevel optimization problems, particularly in scenarios where the upper-level objective function lacks bounded smoothness (i.e., its gradient is not Lipschitz continuous). Such scenarios are relevant in training modern deep neural networks like transformers. The authors propose "AdamBO," an Adam-type algorithm tailored for this setting. AdamBO uses a single-loop approach, updating both upper- and lower-level variables simultaneously, combining Adam updates for the upper level and SGD for the lower level. The key technical contribution is a novel randomness decoupling lemma, which refines control over the lower-level variable and overcomes challenges related to the hypergradient bias. The authors prove an  O(€^{-4}) oracle complexity to find e-stationary points and provide empirical results on meta-learning and deep AUC maximization tasks using RNNs and transformers, demonstrating improved performance compared to existing bilevel optimization algorithms.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's main contribution lies in adapting the Adam optimization strategy to bilevel optimization problems with unbounded smoothness. While Adam itself is well-established, and prior work has addressed bilevel optimization and unbounded smoothness independently, their combination introduces unique analytical challenges. The randomness decoupling lemma appears to be a significant contribution, providing a new technique for analyzing lower-level error control in the context of adaptive optimizers and bilevel problems. The refined analysis of the hypergradient bias also adds to the theoretical depth.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from the practical relevance of bilevel optimization in machine learning, especially for meta-learning, hyperparameter optimization, and other tasks where nested optimization is inherent. Furthermore, the ability to handle unbounded smoothness is crucial for training transformers, which are ubiquitous in modern deep learning. AdamBO, if effective, could provide a valuable tool for training these models in bilevel optimization contexts. The experimental results, showing improved AUC and faster convergence with transformers, strengthen this claim. The algorithm and analysis are very promising.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Addresses a practically relevant problem in modern deep learning.
    *   Provides a rigorous theoretical analysis with a provable convergence guarantee.
    *   Introduces a novel randomness decoupling lemma that has broader applicability.
    *   Demonstrates strong empirical performance compared to existing methods.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The O(€^{-4}) oracle complexity is competitive with existing non-adaptive methods but could potentially be improved. An avenue for further research would be to explore variance reduction techniques to lower the complexity.
    * The O(λ^{-2}) dependence in the constant is very high. The authors acknowledge the potential limitation in practice as λ can be small. Empirical analysis showed the impact to be not too drastic but the bound may not scale to larger models or with different hyperparameters. It weakens the novelty significantly.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence research in bilevel optimization and adaptive optimization. The randomness decoupling lemma could become a standard tool in analyzing similar algorithms. The empirical results are a strong motivation for practitioners to adopt AdamBO in their bilevel optimization tasks.

*   **Justification of Score:** The paper makes a notable contribution by extending Adam to handle bilevel optimization with unbounded smoothness, which is a challenging setting. The introduction of the randomness decoupling lemma is a valuable technique. While the complexity is competitive and a limitation is acknowledged, the practical performance improvements and the theoretical framework developed justify a solid rating.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Safe LLM-Controlled Robots with Formal Guarantees via Reachability Analysis
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.03911v1)
- **Authors**: Ahmad Hafez, Alireza Naderi Akhormeh, Amr Hegazy, Amr Alanwar
- **Abstract**: The deployment of Large Language Models (LLMs) in robotic systems presents unique safety challenges, particularly in unpredictable environments. Although LLMs, leveraging zero-shot learning, enhance human-robot interaction and decision-making capabilities, their inherent probabilistic nature and lack of formal guarantees raise significant concerns for safety-critical applications. Traditional model-based verification approaches often rely on precise system models, which are difficult to obtain for real-world robotic systems and may not be fully trusted due to modeling inaccuracies, unmodeled dynamics, or environmental uncertainties. To address these challenges, this paper introduces a safety assurance framework for LLM-controlled robots based on data-driven reachability analysis, a formal verification technique that ensures all possible system trajectories remain within safe operational limits. Our framework specifically investigates the problem of instructing an LLM to navigate the robot to a specified goal and assesses its ability to generate low-level control actions that successfully guide the robot safely toward that goal. By leveraging historical data to construct reachable sets of states for the robot-LLM system, our approach provides rigorous safety guarantees against unsafe behaviors without relying on explicit analytical models. We validate the framework through experimental case studies in autonomous navigation and task planning, demonstrating its effectiveness in mitigating risks associated with LLM-generated commands. This work advances the integration of formal methods into LLM-based robotics, offering a principled and practical approach to ensuring safety in next-generation autonomous systems.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've reviewed the paper "Safe LLM-Controlled Robots with Formal Guarantees via Reachability Analysis." Here's a summary and critical evaluation:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the safety challenges associated with integrating Large Language Models (LLMs) into robotic systems. It proposes a novel framework that combines data-driven reachability analysis with LLM-based control to provide formal safety guarantees.  The approach uses historical data to construct reachable sets, enabling safety verification without relying on explicit analytical models of the robot's dynamics.  The framework assesses the LLM's ability to generate low-level control actions that safely guide the robot to a goal, mitigating risks from LLM-generated commands. The authors validate their approach through simulations and real experiments in autonomous navigation and task planning scenarios, demonstrating its effectiveness in maintaining safety in unpredictable environments.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of combining data-driven reachability analysis with LLM control is novel. While data-driven reachability analysis and LLM control individually are not new, their integration in this specific context, particularly for safety assurance in robotic systems, is a significant contribution. The use of zero-shot learning with LLMs further enhances the novelty, allowing generalization to unseen tasks and environments. The framework's ability to work with black-box system models, avoiding reliance on potentially inaccurate analytical models, is a particularly valuable feature. However, the novelty might be slightly diminished by the inspiration drawn from previous work, especially the extension of existing reachability analysis techniques.

*   **Significance:** The paper tackles a critical problem in the burgeoning field of LLM-based robotics: ensuring safety. As LLMs become increasingly integrated into robotic systems operating in complex and unpredictable environments, the need for formal safety guarantees becomes paramount. The proposed framework offers a practical and principled approach to address this need, potentially enabling the safe deployment of LLM-controlled robots in safety-critical applications. The experimental validation, including simulations and real-world experiments, strengthens the significance of the work by demonstrating its practical feasibility and effectiveness. However, the paper's significance will ultimately depend on its impact on real-world applications and its ability to scale to more complex robotic systems and environments. Also, the reliance on good offline data is a common limitation of this class of methods.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly defines the safety challenges associated with LLM-controlled robots and the limitations of traditional model-based verification approaches.
    *   **Novel Integration:** The integration of data-driven reachability analysis and LLM control is innovative and addresses a critical gap in the field.
    *   **Practical Feasibility:** The framework is computationally efficient and scalable, making it suitable for real-world deployment.
    *   **Experimental Validation:** The extensive experimental validation, including simulations and real-world experiments, demonstrates the effectiveness of the proposed approach.
    *   **Zero-shot Learning:** The integration of zero-shot learning enables generalization to unseen tasks and environments.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Data Dependency:** The data-driven nature of the approach relies on the availability of sufficient and representative historical data, which might be a limitation in some applications. Quality of data may drastically affect the results.
    *   **Scalability Limitations:** While the paper claims scalability, the experimental validation is limited to relatively simple robotic systems and environments. The scalability of the approach to more complex systems with higher-dimensional state spaces and more intricate dynamics needs further investigation.
    *   **Simplified Obstacle Model:** The assumption of static obstacles might be restrictive in dynamic environments.
    *   **LLM Prompt Sensitivity:** The paper acknowledges the sensitivity of LLMs to prompt wording, which could affect the reliability and consistency of the system. The dependence on the LLM performance can be a weakness if that LLM model is no longer supported in the future.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence the field of LLM-based robotics by providing a practical and principled approach to safety assurance. It could pave the way for the safe deployment of LLM-controlled robots in a wide range of applications, including healthcare, autonomous delivery, and industrial automation. The framework could also inspire further research on integrating formal methods with LLM control and developing more robust and reliable safety assurance techniques.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:**

The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of LLM-based robotics by addressing the critical challenge of safety assurance. The integration of data-driven reachability analysis and LLM control is innovative and offers a practical approach to ensuring safety in complex and unpredictable environments. The experimental validation strengthens the significance of the work by demonstrating its feasibility and effectiveness. However, the limitations related to data dependency, scalability, simplified obstacle models, and LLM prompt sensitivity, prevent it from receiving a higher score. The paper is a valuable contribution and has the potential to influence future research and development in the field.

- **Classification**: cs.RO
- **Score**: 7/10

### GuardDoor: Safeguarding Against Malicious Diffusion Editing via Protective Backdoors
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.03944v1)
- **Authors**: Yaopei Zeng, Yuanpu Cao, Lu Lin
- **Abstract**: The growing accessibility of diffusion models has revolutionized image editing but also raised significant concerns about unauthorized modifications, such as misinformation and plagiarism. Existing countermeasures largely rely on adversarial perturbations designed to disrupt diffusion model outputs. However, these approaches are found to be easily neutralized by simple image preprocessing techniques, such as compression and noise addition. To address this limitation, we propose GuardDoor, a novel and robust protection mechanism that fosters collaboration between image owners and model providers. Specifically, the model provider participating in the mechanism fine-tunes the image encoder to embed a protective backdoor, allowing image owners to request the attachment of imperceptible triggers to their images. When unauthorized users attempt to edit these protected images with this diffusion model, the model produces meaningless outputs, reducing the risk of malicious image editing. Our method demonstrates enhanced robustness against image preprocessing operations and is scalable for large-scale deployment. This work underscores the potential of cooperative frameworks between model providers and image owners to safeguard digital content in the era of generative AI.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "GuardDoor: Safeguarding Against Malicious Diffusion Editing via Protective Backdoors":

**Summary:**
