Inspired by human reading behavior, TextDoctor restores fundamental text elements from patches and then applies diffusion models to entire document images instead of training models on specific document types. To handle varying text sizes and avoid out-of-memory issues, common in high-resolution documents, we propose using structure pyramid prediction and patch pyramid diffusion models. These techniques leverage multiscale inputs and pyramid patches to enhance the quality of inpainting both globally and locally. Extensive qualitative and quantitative experiments on seven public datasets validated that TextDoctor outperforms state-of-the-art methods in restoring various types of high-resolution document images.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "TextDoctor: Unified Document Image Inpainting via Patch Pyramid Diffusion Models":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces TextDoctor, a novel approach to document image inpainting that aims to address the challenges of generalization to unseen document styles and handling high-resolution images. The method leverages the observation that fine-grained text patches share visual similarities across different document types.  TextDoctor trains diffusion-based inpainting models on corrupted image patches containing basic text elements and then applies them to entire document images. To handle varying text sizes and high-resolution images, the paper proposes structure pyramid prediction (PSPP) to capture structural features at multiple scales, and patch pyramid diffusion models (CPPD) to inpaint the entire document image in a patch-wise manner using these structural details. The authors demonstrate the effectiveness of TextDoctor through extensive experiments on various public datasets.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of training diffusion models on text patches for document inpainting is interesting. The key novelty lies in the architecture (PSPP, CPPD) that enables handling high-resolution images without running out of memory and improving the quality of the inpainting by capturing both local and global features. The approach is inspired by human reading behavior, which makes the design choice justifiable. However, while combining different ideas, the proposed method does not necessarily propose a paradigm shifting approach for image inpainting.

*   **Significance:** Document image inpainting is a practical and relevant problem, and the paper addresses some limitations of existing methods, particularly generalization and scalability to high-resolution images. The proposed TextDoctor demonstrates improved performance compared to several SOTA methods. The authors provide extensive qualitative and quantitative results to validate their approach. The experimental results are convincing, and the improvement over other inpainting methods especially given that other methods are generally dataset specific, demonstrates a level of generalization.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The patch-based approach is effective for handling high-resolution images, mitigating memory constraints.
    *   The structure pyramid prediction helps capture multi-scale information, leading to improved inpainting quality.
    *   The proposed method shows good generalization ability across different document types.
    *   Comprehensive experiments on a range of datasets validate the effectiveness of TextDoctor.
    *   The paper is well-written and clearly explains the proposed method and experimental setup.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   While the combination of existing techniques (diffusion models, patch-based processing, pyramid representations) is effective, the individual components are not entirely novel in themselves.
    *   The complexity of the architecture adds computational overhead. Although the paper addresses memory issues it acknowledges the introduced latency. A more detailed analysis of the time complexity relative to the quality improvement would strengthen the claims.
    *   The paper mentions limitations regarding large masks. This indicates that the method struggles with recovering large missing portions.
    *   The reliance on an accurate structure prediction network is a potential bottleneck, as errors in the predicted structure can negatively impact inpainting quality. It would strengthen the paper if they investigated and addressed errors in the predicted structure.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence future research on document image inpainting, particularly in the areas of high-resolution processing and generalization. The patch pyramid approach and the use of diffusion models with multi-scale features are promising directions. The code will definitely increase its impact, although whether this will be significant is difficult to predict.

**Justification for the Score:**

The paper presents a well-engineered solution to a relevant problem with promising results. While the core components (diffusion, patching, pyramid) are not radically new, the way they are integrated to create a unified and scalable document inpainting framework demonstrates valuable engineering insight. The approach has the potential to be useful in real-world applications, especially those involving high-resolution documents. The method’s reliance on a precise structure prediction model and the fact it involves a significant computational overhead limits the rating to the upper half of the scale. Considering its strengths, it is a well engineered approach with novelty in its combined approach.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Robust Data Watermarking in Language Models by Injecting Fictitious Knowledge
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04036v1)
- **Authors**: Xinyue Cui, Johnny Tian-Zheng Wei, Swabha Swayamdipta, Robin Jia
- **Abstract**: Data watermarking in language models injects traceable signals, such as specific token sequences or stylistic patterns, into copyrighted text, allowing copyright holders to track and verify training data ownership. Previous data watermarking techniques primarily focus on effective memorization after pretraining, while overlooking challenges that arise in other stages of the LLM pipeline, such as the risk of watermark filtering during data preprocessing, or potential forgetting through post-training, or verification difficulties due to API-only access. We propose a novel data watermarking approach that injects coherent and plausible yet fictitious knowledge into training data using generated passages describing a fictitious entity and its associated attributes. Our watermarks are designed to be memorized by the LLM through seamlessly integrating in its training data, making them harder to detect lexically during preprocessing.We demonstrate that our watermarks can be effectively memorized by LLMs, and that increasing our watermarks' density, length, and diversity of attributes strengthens their memorization. We further show that our watermarks remain robust throughout LLM development, maintaining their effectiveness after continual pretraining and supervised finetuning. Finally, we show that our data watermarks can be evaluated even under API-only access via question answering.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and evaluation of the paper:

**Summary**

The paper proposes a novel data watermarking technique for language models (LLMs) that injects fictitious, yet plausible, knowledge into the training data. This method aims to overcome limitations of existing watermarking approaches, which are vulnerable to data filtering, forgetting during post-training, and verification difficulties with API-only access to LLMs. The authors construct watermarks based on semantic frames from FrameNet, generating plausible entity names and associated attributes. They demonstrate that these watermarks are effectively memorized by LLMs, robust to various data filtering techniques (including adversarial filtering), and retain effectiveness after continual pre-training and supervised fine-tuning. They also propose a question-answering-based method for watermark verification, suitable even with API-only access.  Key factors influencing watermark strength such as size, length, number of attributes, injection strategies, and linguistic diversity are explored empirically.

**Critical Evaluation**

**Novelty:** The idea of using fictitious knowledge as watermarks is a significant contribution. Existing techniques often rely on repetitive patterns or token sequences, which are easily detectable and removable. Injecting semantically plausible, but fabricated, information provides a level of stealth not present in earlier methods.  The QA-based verification is a useful contribution for scenarios with API-only access.  The analysis of different watermark design factors and their influence on memorization strength is also valuable and methodically presented.

**Significance:**  The work addresses a crucial problem in the LLM space: protecting copyright and verifying data ownership. The proposed method's robustness to data filtering and post-training effects directly tackles the limitations of current watermarking solutions. The QA-based evaluation is particularly relevant given the increasing prevalence of closed-source LLMs.  The exploration of different watermark design considerations offers practical guidance for implementing such systems. The emphasis on stealth (evasion of filtering) alongside robustness (persisting after training) makes this a practically relevant contribution.

**Strengths:**

*   **Novel approach:** The core idea of fictitious knowledge injection is a creative solution.
*   **Comprehensive analysis:** The paper thoroughly investigates various aspects of the proposed method, including watermark design, robustness, and verification.
*   **Practical relevance:** The work addresses real-world challenges in LLM watermarking, such as data filtering and API-only access.
*   **Empirical validation:** The method is rigorously evaluated through experiments, providing evidence for its effectiveness. The experimental setup is well-defined and controlled. The use of hypothesis testing to quantify memorization strength is appropriate.
*   **Adversarial robustness:**  The explicit consideration of adversarial attacks (deduplication filtering) significantly strengthens the work.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Proxy Evaluation:** The evaluation heavily relies on smaller models (Pythia-160M, Olmo-7B) and limited dataset sizes (100M-1B tokens). While the authors acknowledge this limitation, scaling these results to truly large-scale LLMs and datasets remains a question. The degree to which findings generalize isn't certain.
*   **Scalability of Generation:** Creating diverse and semantically plausible fictitious knowledge requires significant effort, and its scalability to a vast range of potential watermarks needs further consideration. Although the methodology leverages LLMs for much of the process, the curation of frame elements remains a bottleneck.
*   **Ethical Considerations:** While the authors address ethical considerations in the generated watermarks, the potential for malicious actors to exploit this technique for spreading misinformation (injecting false facts) needs careful consideration. This is a general issue with any knowledge injection, however.
*   **Dependence on FrameNet:**  While FrameNet provides a solid foundation, it is not exhaustive. Expanding the knowledge base would increase the method's applicability.
*   **Limited Attack Surface:** While the study shows robustness to deduplication filtering, the vulnerability to other potential attacks, such as model extraction or watermark removal techniques tailored to knowledge-based watermarks, isn't thoroughly explored.

**Justification of Score:**

Considering the novelty of the approach, the significance of the problem addressed, the comprehensive analysis, and the practical relevance of the results, but also accounting for the proxy evaluation limitations and other weaknesses listed above, a score of **7** is justified. The paper makes a valuable contribution to the field of LLM watermarking by introducing a novel and robust technique. However, the limitations related to the scale of evaluation and some aspects of real-world applicability prevent a higher score. The work provides a strong foundation for future research and development in this area, and its impact will likely be significant as LLMs become increasingly prevalent. It offers a genuinely new direction, with potential for significant adoption.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 7/10

### Beyond Existance: Fulfill 3D Reconstructed Scenes with Pseudo Details
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04037v1)
- **Authors**: Yifei Gao, Jun Huang, Lei Wang, Ruiting Dai, Jun Cheng
- **Abstract**: The emergence of 3D Gaussian Splatting (3D-GS) has significantly advanced 3D reconstruction by providing high fidelity and fast training speeds across various scenarios. While recent efforts have mainly focused on improving model structures to compress data volume or reduce artifacts during zoom-in and zoom-out operations, they often overlook an underlying issue: training sampling deficiency. In zoomed-in views, Gaussian primitives can appear unregulated and distorted due to their dilation limitations and the insufficient availability of scale-specific training samples. Consequently, incorporating pseudo-details that ensure the completeness and alignment of the scene becomes essential. In this paper, we introduce a new training method that integrates diffusion models and multi-scale training using pseudo-ground-truth data. This approach not only notably mitigates the dilation and zoomed-in artifacts but also enriches reconstructed scenes with precise details out of existing scenarios. Our method achieves state-of-the-art performance across various benchmarks and extends the capabilities of 3D reconstruction beyond training datasets.
- **Summary**: Here is a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Beyond Existence: Fulfill 3D Reconstructed Scenes with Pseudo Details":

**Summary**

The paper addresses the issue of training sample deficiency in 3D Gaussian Splatting (3D-GS), particularly concerning zoomed-in views where Gaussian primitives can appear distorted due to dilation limitations. To combat this, the authors propose a new training method that integrates diffusion models and multi-scale training, leveraging pseudo-ground-truth data. This approach aims to mitigate dilation and zoom-in artifacts, enriching reconstructed scenes with precise details that are otherwise absent. The authors claim state-of-the-art performance across benchmarks and extended capabilities beyond the original training datasets. The approach involves upscaling and refining details using diffusion models, while maintaining consistency between high-frequency and low-frequency data.  A refined bootstrapping pipeline is also introduced.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Novelty:** The integration of upscale diffusion models with 3D-GS to address training sample deficiency and improve zoomed-in view rendering is a significant contribution. While other methods have attempted to address artifacts and redundancy in 3D-GS, the specific use of diffusion models for detail enrichment from a zoomed-in perspective appears novel. The refined bootstrapping pipeline also adds some value, albeit a smaller contribution.

*   **Significance:** The paper tackles a practical problem in 3D reconstruction - the loss of fidelity and emergence of artifacts during zoom-in operations. By improving detail and reducing artifacts, the proposed method could have a real impact on applications such as VR, AR, and scene navigation where users frequently interact with scenes at varying zoom levels.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper clearly identifies a specific and important problem in existing 3D-GS methods.
    *   The proposed solution is technically sound and well-motivated, leveraging the strengths of diffusion models for detail generation.
    *   The combination of upscaling, diffusion models, and a refined bootstrapping pipeline shows a thorough approach to the problem.
    *   The experimental results demonstrate significant improvements in performance compared to existing methods, including the mitigation of zoom-in artifacts.
    *   The paper is well-written and easy to follow.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the paper claims state-of-the-art performance, the metrics presented may not fully capture the perceptual improvements achieved, especially in detail enrichment. More human studies may be necessary to support the claims about visual quality.
    *   The experiments, although extensive, could be more focused on evaluating the benefits of the individual components of the proposed method (upscaling, diffusion models, bootstrapping). Ablation studies, while included, could be more insightful.
    *   The method introduces additional complexity and training time. While the authors refine the bootstrapping process, it may still be a significant overhead.
    *   The reliance on diffusion models introduces the potential for biases and artifacts inherent in these models, which could affect the realism and accuracy of the reconstructed details.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in 3D reconstruction, particularly regarding methods for improving detail and reducing artifacts in neural rendering techniques. The integration of diffusion models for detail generation could inspire new approaches to scene completion and enhancement. The refined bootstrapping technique may help improve training stability.

*   **Score Justification:** The paper presents a novel and technically sound approach to a practical problem in 3D reconstruction. It offers clear improvements in performance and has the potential to influence future research in the field. However, the reliance on specific metrics and the computational overhead somewhat limit the impact. While significant, further user-based experiments and more analysis about the failure modes of the method are needed.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.GR
- **Score**: 7/10

### Underlying Semantic Diffusion for Effective and Efficient In-Context Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04050v1)
- **Authors**: Zhong Ji, Weilong Cao, Yan Zhang, Yanwei Pang, Jungong Han, Xuelong Li
- **Abstract**: Diffusion models has emerged as a powerful framework for tasks like image controllable generation and dense prediction. However, existing models often struggle to capture underlying semantics (e.g., edges, textures, shapes) and effectively utilize in-context learning, limiting their contextual understanding and image generation quality. Additionally, high computational costs and slow inference speeds hinder their real-time applicability. To address these challenges, we propose Underlying Semantic Diffusion (US-Diffusion), an enhanced diffusion model that boosts underlying semantics learning, computational efficiency, and in-context learning capabilities on multi-task scenarios. We introduce Separate & Gather Adapter (SGA), which decouples input conditions for different tasks while sharing the architecture, enabling better in-context learning and generalization across diverse visual domains. We also present a Feedback-Aided Learning (FAL) framework, which leverages feedback signals to guide the model in capturing semantic details and dynamically adapting to task-specific contextual cues. Furthermore, we propose a plug-and-play Efficient Sampling Strategy (ESS) for dense sampling at time steps with high-noise levels, which aims at optimizing training and inference efficiency while maintaining strong in-context learning performance. Experimental results demonstrate that US-Diffusion outperforms the state-of-the-art method, achieving an average reduction of 7.47 in FID on Map2Image tasks and an average reduction of 0.026 in RMSE on Image2Map tasks, while achieving approximately 9.45 times faster inference speed. Our method also demonstrates superior training efficiency and in-context learning capabilities, excelling in new datasets and tasks, highlighting its robustness and adaptability across diverse visual domains.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Underlying Semantic Diffusion for Effective and Efficient In-Context Learning":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces US-Diffusion, a diffusion model designed to enhance in-context learning in computer vision. It addresses the limitations of existing diffusion models in capturing underlying semantics, computational efficiency, and in-context learning abilities across diverse tasks. US-Diffusion incorporates three key components: a Separate & Gather Adapter (SGA) for better handling of task-specific contexts, a Feedback-Aided Learning (FAL) framework that uses feedback signals to guide semantic detail capture, and an Efficient Sampling Strategy (ESS) to optimize training and inference efficiency by dense sampling at high-noise time steps. The authors demonstrate that US-Diffusion outperforms state-of-the-art methods in Map2Image and Image2Map tasks, exhibiting superior training efficiency and generalization capabilities.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The combination of SGA, FAL, and ESS represents a significant attempt to improve both the quality and efficiency of diffusion models, particularly in the context of in-context learning.
    *   **SGA Contribution**: The Separate & Gather Adapter (SGA) is presented as a novel way to better leverage contextual cues and learn task specific features.
    *   **FAL Contribution:**  Leveraging a Feedback-Aided Learning (FAL) framework to incorporate visual feedback is novel in a diffusion model setting; this approach facilitates the fine-tuning process of the model.
    *   **ESS Contribution:** Proposing an efficient sampling strategy to accelerate the model makes it attractive to users concerned with the efficiency and speed of their models.

*   **Significance:** The study addresses critical limitations of diffusion models in computer vision, particularly the difficulty in capturing fine-grained semantics and computational burdens. The reported improvements in FID, RMSE, and inference speed position US-Diffusion as a significant advancement, with potential impact for both image generation and dense prediction tasks.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The design of US-Diffusion is well-motivated, addressing key bottlenecks in existing diffusion models.
    *   The experimental results demonstrate significant quantitative improvements over existing methods.
    *   The ablation studies effectively highlight the contribution of each component (SGA, FAL, and ESS).
    *   The qualitative results visually demonstrate that US-Diffusion aligns better with the intended spatial details.
    *   The demonstration of generalization ability to novel tasks (e.g., Canny-to-Image, Scribble-to-Image) increases the credibility of the contribution.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The improvements on some Image2Map tasks were more modest. Some experimental results highlight possible dependence on training set selection.
    *   While the results show that our model performs better on novel tasks than PromptDiff, the evaluation metric RMSE and FID is not sufficient to demonstrate this performance increase.

*   **Justification for Score:** While the individual components might draw inspiration from existing techniques, their integrated application within a diffusion model, specifically designed to enhance in-context learning while retaining efficiency, represents a valuable contribution to the field. There is potential for extending its application to other vision tasks and datasets.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### RA-DP: Rapid Adaptive Diffusion Policy for Training-Free High-frequency Robotics Replanning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04051v1)
- **Authors**: Xi Ye, Rui Heng Yang, Jun Jin, Yinchuan Li, Amir Rasouli
- **Abstract**: Diffusion models exhibit impressive scalability in robotic task learning, yet they struggle to adapt to novel, highly dynamic environments. This limitation primarily stems from their constrained replanning ability: they either operate at a low frequency due to a time-consuming iterative sampling process, or are unable to adapt to unforeseen feedback in case of rapid replanning. To address these challenges, we propose RA-DP, a novel diffusion policy framework with training-free high-frequency replanning ability that solves the above limitations in adapting to unforeseen dynamic environments. Specifically, our method integrates guidance signals which are often easily obtained in the new environment during the diffusion sampling process, and utilizes a novel action queue mechanism to generate replanned actions at every denoising step without retraining, thus forming a complete training-free framework for robot motion adaptation in unseen environments. Extensive evaluations have been conducted in both well-recognized simulation benchmarks and real robot tasks. Results show that RA-DP outperforms the state-of-the-art diffusion-based methods in terms of replanning frequency and success rate. Moreover, we show that our framework is theoretically compatible with any training-free guidance signal.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces RA-DP, a novel diffusion policy framework designed to enhance the replanning frequency of robot controllers in dynamic environments.  RA-DP tackles limitations of existing diffusion-based methods, which often struggle to adapt to unforeseen feedback due to low replanning rates or inability to generalize to new environmental conditions. RA-DP integrates training-free loss-based guidance, allowing the policy to adapt to various conditional inputs encoded as scalar differential loss functions *without* retraining. It also introduces an action queue mechanism, inspired by human motion synthesis, that maintains actions with varying noise levels for high-frequency control in dynamic environments, enabling replanning at every denoising step. The method is evaluated in simulations and real-world robot tasks, demonstrating improved replanning frequency and success rate compared to state-of-the-art diffusion policies.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:**  The paper presents several novel components, making it a significant advancement over existing diffusion policy methods.
    *   **Training-Free Adaptation:**  The use of training-free loss-based guidance signals *is* a relatively established concept, but its seamless integration *within* the diffusion policy framework for real-time robotic control is a valuable contribution.  Previous work focused primarily on static image generation or specific conditional modalities (which require pre-training specialized discriminators). The RA-DP method removes the constraint and significantly improves practical application.

    *   **Action Queue with Varying Noise Levels:**  The action queue mechanism, with actions perturbed by different noise levels, is a creative adaptation of human motion synthesis principles to robotics.  This component directly addresses the real-time constraint by enabling high-frequency replanning without sacrificing the inherent sampling quality of diffusion models. The idea of applying guidance at each denoising step is potentially significant.

    *   **Overall Integration:**  While individual components might draw inspiration from existing works, the *combination* of these elements into a cohesive, high-frequency, adaptive diffusion policy framework represents a key novelty. This integration addresses a critical gap in the application of diffusion models to real-world robotics.

* **Significance:**  The paper's significance lies in improving the *practicality* of diffusion-based robot controllers.  The ability to replan at high frequencies and adapt to dynamic, unseen conditions significantly expands the application domain of diffusion policies.

    *   **Real-World Applicability:**  The real-robot experiments validate the claims about robustness and generalizability. The ability to interact with human-modified environments and static/dynamic environments demonstrates practical applicability. The experiments are well-designed and the results are clearly presented and interpreted.

    *   **Overcoming Limitations:**  The paper demonstrably addresses existing limitations of diffusion models in robotics. The core challenge of balancing sampling quality, replanning frequency and adaptation is addressed. The work goes beyond theoretical improvements, providing concrete empirical evidence of the benefits.

* **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper provides thorough evaluations in both simulated and real-world settings, comparing against strong baselines and including ablation studies to justify design choices.
    *   **Clear Writing and Presentation:** The proposed method is well-explained with clear figures and algorithms, making it easy to understand.
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** The experimental results consistently show the superiority of RA-DP over existing methods in terms of replanning frequency and success rate.

* **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Guidance Dependence:** Performance is strongly tied to the effectiveness of the guidance signal (loss function). If this function is poorly designed or not informative enough, the adaptation capabilities of RA-DP will be limited.
    *   **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** Fine-tuning hyperparameters (step size, noise schedule, etc.) may be crucial for optimal performance. This could add overhead to implementation.
    *   **Computational Cost:** While it increases the replanning frequency, the computational cost involved in the high-frequency guidance gradient computation is not explicitly addressed and may become limiting in more complex environments.
    *   **Theoretical Framework:** The work lacks a more formal mathematical treatment of the convergence or stability properties of the guidance scheme within the diffusion framework.

* **Potential Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in diffusion-based robotic control, making it a more viable option for complex, real-time applications. It provides a solid foundation for further exploration of training-free adaptation techniques.

**Justification for Score:**

RA-DP makes substantial contributions to improving the *practicality* and *adaptability* of diffusion policies for real-world robotics, particularly in fast-changing environments, while building on established training free guidance methods. The combination of the action queue mechanism and training-free guidance, along with comprehensive experimental validation, justifies a high score. However, the dependence on guidance signal design and potential sensitivity to hyperparameters somewhat reduces the score.

Score: 8.5

- **Classification**: cs.RO
- **Score**: 8/10

### Uncovering inequalities in new knowledge learning by large language models across different languages
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04064v1)
- **Authors**: Chenglong Wang, Haoyu Tang, Xiyuan Yang, Yueqi Xie, Jina Suh, Sunayana Sitaram, Junming Huang, Yu Xie, Zhaoya Gong, Xing Xie, Fangzhao Wu
- **Abstract**: As large language models (LLMs) gradually become integral tools for problem solving in daily life worldwide, understanding linguistic inequality is becoming increasingly important. Existing research has primarily focused on static analyses that assess the disparities in the existing knowledge and capabilities of LLMs across languages. However, LLMs are continuously evolving, acquiring new knowledge to generate up-to-date, domain-specific responses. Investigating linguistic inequalities within this dynamic process is, therefore, also essential. In this paper, we explore inequalities in new knowledge learning by LLMs across different languages and four key dimensions: effectiveness, transferability, prioritization, and robustness. Through extensive experiments under two settings (in-context learning and fine-tuning) using both proprietary and open-source models, we demonstrate that low-resource languages consistently face disadvantages across all four dimensions. By shedding light on these disparities, we aim to raise awareness of linguistic inequalities in LLMs' new knowledge learning, fostering the development of more inclusive and equitable future LLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Uncovering inequalities in new knowledge learning by large language models across different languages":

**Summary:**

This paper investigates how effectively Large Language Models (LLMs) learn new knowledge across different languages, focusing on inequalities between high-resource and low-resource languages. The authors examine effectiveness, transferability, prioritization (handling conflicting information), and robustness (resistance to incorrect information) in two learning settings: in-context learning and fine-tuning. They use both proprietary (GPT-4o-mini) and open-source (Llama-3.1-8B) models, testing them on newly created multilingual datasets of fictional and common-sense knowledge. The study reveals that LLMs consistently struggle to learn new knowledge in low-resource languages as well, showing poorer performance and transferability. When knowledge conflicts arise, LLMs prioritize information from high-resource languages. They are also more susceptible to incorporating incorrect information when processing low-resource languages. The paper concludes that significant linguistic inequalities exist in LLMs' ability to acquire new knowledge, highlighting the need for responsible and inclusive AI development.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Important Problem:** The paper addresses a crucial and often overlooked issue: linguistic bias in LLMs' ability to learn *new* information. Most research focuses on existing knowledge, making this a timely and valuable contribution.
    *   **Comprehensive Framework:**  The defined framework incorporating effectiveness, transferability, prioritization, and robustness provides a structured and comprehensive approach to evaluating inequalities.
    *   **Controlled Experimental Design:** The use of carefully constructed multilingual datasets, including fictional knowledge, strengthens the experimental validity. It is crucial to use the fictional knowledge, since the existing knowledge is likely not to be evenly distributed, and that the fictional one will be unseen before.
    *   **Use of Multiple Models:** Testing on both proprietary and open-source models increases the generalizability of the findings.
    *   **Clear Results and Discussion:**  The paper presents its findings clearly and discusses their implications thoroughly, highlighting the ethical and societal concerns surrounding linguistic bias.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Language Selection:** While the paper includes both high-resource and low-resource languages and attempts linguistic diversity, the number of languages (17) could be expanded for greater robustness, although this would significantly increase computational cost.
    *   **Reliance on Machine Translation:** The reliance on Google Translate for creating multilingual datasets introduces a potential source of error or bias, even with quality checks. Better cross-lingual datasets will be preferable if possible.
    *   **Domain of Fictional Knowledge:** It is not clear whether the result is generalizable to some real-world knowledge learning.
    *   **Specificity of Learning Methods:** The study focuses primarily on in-context learning and fine-tuning. While these are important methods, exploring other learning paradigms could provide a more complete picture.
    *   **Statistical Significance Tests:** The absence of statistical significance tests on the results reported across languages is a significant oversight. While trends are visually apparent, the lack of statistical analysis makes it difficult to definitively conclude that the observed differences are not due to random chance.

*   **Novelty and Significance:** The paper's main novelty lies in shifting the focus from static analysis of LLM knowledge to the dynamic process of new knowledge acquisition and the discovery of systematic inequalities in this process. Its significance is that it highlights a hidden dimension of bias in LLMs that directly impacts users of low-resource languages, leading to potentially unequal access to information and functionality.

*   **Potential Influence:** This work is likely to influence future research in multilingual LLMs, inspiring the development of techniques that promote more equitable knowledge learning. The four dimensions examined in the paper can become a standard framework for evaluations.

**Justification:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution to understanding linguistic bias in LLMs, especially its effects on new knowledge acquisition. The methodical evaluation and clear presentation of results are strengths. However, several weaknesses, mainly the lack of statistical significance tests, limit the robustness of the findings and the strength of claims made. Future work should prioritize a more robust statistical analysis to provide stronger support for the conclusions.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### FREAK: Frequency-modulated High-fidelity and Real-time Audio-driven Talking Portrait Synthesis
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04067v1)
- **Authors**: Ziqi Ni, Ao Fu, Yi Zhou
- **Abstract**: Achieving high-fidelity lip-speech synchronization in audio-driven talking portrait synthesis remains challenging. While multi-stage pipelines or diffusion models yield high-quality results, they suffer from high computational costs. Some approaches perform well on specific individuals with low resources, yet still exhibit mismatched lip movements. The aforementioned methods are modeled in the pixel domain. We observed that there are noticeable discrepancies in the frequency domain between the synthesized talking videos and natural videos. Currently, no research on talking portrait synthesis has considered this aspect. To address this, we propose a FREquency-modulated, high-fidelity, and real-time Audio-driven talKing portrait synthesis framework, named FREAK, which models talking portraits from the frequency domain perspective, enhancing the fidelity and naturalness of the synthesized portraits. FREAK introduces two novel frequency-based modules: 1) the Visual Encoding Frequency Modulator (VEFM) to couple multi-scale visual features in the frequency domain, better preserving visual frequency information and reducing the gap in the frequency spectrum between synthesized and natural frames. and 2) the Audio Visual Frequency Modulator (AVFM) to help the model learn the talking pattern in the frequency domain and improve audio-visual synchronization. Additionally, we optimize the model in both pixel domain and frequency domain jointly. Furthermore, FREAK supports seamless switching between one-shot and video dubbing settings, offering enhanced flexibility. Due to its superior performance, it can simultaneously support high-resolution video results and real-time inference. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our method synthesizes high-fidelity talking portraits with detailed facial textures and precise lip synchronization in real-time, outperforming state-of-the-art methods.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes FREAK, a frequency-modulated framework for high-fidelity, real-time audio-driven talking portrait synthesis.  FREAK addresses the problem of mismatched lip movements and visual quality degradation often observed in existing methods. It introduces two novel frequency-domain modules: the Visual Encoding Frequency Modulator (VEFM) which adaptively couples multi-scale visual features in the frequency domain to preserve visual frequency information, and the Audio Visual Frequency Modulator (AVFM) that modulates visual features with audio embeddings in the frequency domain to improve audio-visual synchronization. The model is optimized jointly in the pixel and frequency domains and supports seamless switching between one-shot and video dubbing settings. Extensive experiments demonstrate that FREAK synthesizes high-fidelity talking portraits with detailed textures and precise lip synchronization in real-time.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its adoption of frequency-domain analysis for audio-driven talking head generation. Existing methods primarily operate in the pixel domain, neglecting frequency-related discrepancies.  The two proposed modules, VEFM and AVFM, represent a novel approach to integrating visual and audio information in the frequency domain to improve the quality and realism of synthesized talking heads.  The flexible switching between one-shot and video dubbing settings is also a nice addition, though perhaps not as fundamentally novel.

* **Significance:**  The problem of achieving realistic, synchronized, and real-time audio-driven talking head synthesis is significant. FREAK tackles this challenge by explicitly addressing frequency-domain issues. The results demonstrate improvements in visual quality (FID, FVD) and lip synchronization (LMD, LSE-D, LSE-C), suggesting that the frequency-domain approach is promising. The real-time performance is crucial for practical applications. The user study provides further evidence of the method's effectiveness from a subjective perspective.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Frequency Domain Approach:**  The core contribution is the exploration of frequency-domain techniques for this task.  This is a relatively unexplored area and the paper makes a convincing case for its effectiveness.
    * **Module Design:** The VEFM and AVFM modules are well-motivated and designed to address specific limitations of pixel-domain approaches.
    * **Quantitative Results:** The extensive experiments with various metrics provide strong evidence for the superiority of FREAK compared to state-of-the-art methods.  Ablation studies clearly show the contributions of each module.
    * **Qualitative Results:** Visual results are of high-fidelity and visually compelling.
    * **Real-time Performance:** FREAK achieves real-time performance while maintaining high resolution.
    * **Seamless switching:** The approach naturally allows seamless switching between a one-shot setting and a video dubbing setting.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Dependency on Hubert Features:** The method relies on pre-trained Hubert features for audio encoding.  While Hubert is a strong baseline, the specific choice and impact of this dependency are not fully explored.  It would be helpful to know how the method performs with other audio feature extractors.
    * **Limited Discussion of Limitations:** While the paper mentions potential failures with short training videos or lack of lip shape variety, a more thorough discussion of limitations would be beneficial.  How does the method handle significant head pose variations? What are the limitations in terms of the speaker's ethnicity or speaking style given the training data?
    * **Broader Impact Discussion:**  The discussion of broader impacts is brief.  Given the potential for misuse of talking head synthesis technology, a more detailed analysis of ethical considerations and potential safeguards would be valuable.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in audio-driven talking head synthesis by highlighting the importance of frequency-domain considerations.  The proposed VEFM and AVFM modules can serve as building blocks for other researchers in the field.  The real-time performance opens up new possibilities for interactive applications.

**Justification of Score:**

I am assigning a score of 7.5. The paper presents a novel approach to audio-driven talking head synthesis by incorporating frequency-domain analysis. The proposed modules (VEFM and AVFM) are well-designed and contribute significantly to improving visual quality and lip synchronization. The quantitative and qualitative results are compelling, demonstrating the superiority of FREAK compared to existing methods. The real-time performance and seamless switching capabilities are also strong assets.

However, there are some limitations. The reliance on pre-trained Hubert features and limited discussion on limitations in terms of various factors like pose, identity, and speaking style prevents a higher score. Also, the discussion of broader impacts and ethical concerns could be more extensive. These factors limit the paper's overall impact to a certain extent. Nevertheless, the core idea of frequency-domain learning is innovative and well-executed, warranting a high score.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Beyond Memorization: Evaluating the True Type Inference Capabilities of LLMs for Java Code Snippets
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04076v1)
- **Authors**: Yiwen Dong, Zhenyang Xu, Yongqiang Tian, Chengnian Sun
- **Abstract**: Type inference is a crucial task for reusing online code snippets, often found on platforms like StackOverflow, which frequently lack essential type information such as fully qualified names (FQNs) and required libraries. Recent studies have leveraged Large Language Models (LLMs) for type inference on code snippets, showing promising results. However, these results are potentially affected by data leakage, as the benchmark suite (StatType-SO) has been public on GitHub since 2017 (full suite in 2023). Thus, it is uncertain whether LLMs' strong performance reflects genuine code semantics understanding or a mere retrieval of ground truth from training data. To comprehensively assess LLMs' type inference capabilities on Java code snippets, we conducted a three-pronged evaluation. First, utilizing Thalia, a program synthesis technique, we created ThaliaType--a new, unseen dataset for type inference evaluation. On unseen snippets, LLM performance dropped significantly, with up to a 59% decrease in precision and 72% in recall. Second, we developed semantic-preserving transformations that significantly degraded LLMs' type inference performance, revealing weaknesses in understanding code semantics. Third, we used delta debugging to identify the minimal syntax elements sufficient for LLM inference. While type inference primarily involves inferring FQNs for types in the code snippet, LLMs correctly infer FQNs even when the types were absent from the snippets, suggesting a reliance on knowledge from training instead of thoroughly analyzing the snippets. Our findings indicate that LLMs' strong past performance likely stemmed from data leakage, rather than a genuine understanding of the semantics of code snippets. Our findings highlight the crucial need for carefully designed benchmarks using unseen code snippets to assess the true capabilities of LLMs for type inference tasks.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've reviewed the paper "Beyond Memorization: Evaluating the True Type Inference Capabilities of LLMs for Java Code Snippets." Here's a summary and critical evaluation:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses concerns about data leakage in the evaluation of Large Language Models (LLMs) for Java type inference. Previous studies showed promising performance, but the benchmark dataset used (StatType-SO) has been publicly available for a long time, raising the possibility that LLMs are simply memorizing solutions rather than genuinely understanding code semantics. The authors conduct a three-pronged evaluation: (1) creating a new, unseen dataset (ThaliaType) using program synthesis, (2) performing semantic-preserving code transformations to test LLM understanding, and (3) using delta debugging to find the minimal code elements necessary for LLM inference. The results show that LLMs perform significantly worse on the unseen dataset and transformed code, and often rely on superficial syntactic patterns rather than deep semantic analysis. The authors conclude that prior evaluations were likely influenced by data leakage and call for carefully designed, rigorously evaluated benchmarks that are explicitly excluded from LLM training data.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary strength of the paper is its focus on *data leakage*. While the general issue is known in machine learning, its *explicit and empirical investigation* within the specific context of LLMs for *Java type inference* is a valuable contribution. The creation of ThaliaType is also a novel aspect, offering a new resource for the community. The semantic transformations and delta debugging are solid methodological choices, but not entirely novel in themselves, they've been effectively adapted.
*   **Significance:** The paper's findings have significant implications for how LLMs are evaluated in software engineering tasks. By demonstrating the potential for inflated performance due to data leakage, it urges researchers to be much more critical of benchmark selection and evaluation methodologies. This is crucial for ensuring that LLMs are genuinely improving in their ability to understand and reason about code, rather than just memorizing patterns. The new ThaliaType dataset and the analysis provide a pathway for more robust evaluations.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Well-defined Research Questions:** The three research questions are clear, focused, and logically build upon each other.
    *   **Rigorous Methodology:** The authors employ a combination of techniques (program synthesis, semantic transformations, delta debugging) to thoroughly investigate the research questions.
    *   **Empirical Evidence:** The paper presents clear and compelling experimental results that support the conclusions. The comparisons between LLMs and SnR are particularly insightful.
    *   **Clear Writing and Presentation:** The paper is well-written, easy to follow, and clearly explains the experimental setup and results.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope of Transformations:** While the semantic-preserving transformations are a good start, more diverse and sophisticated transformations could further probe the depth of LLM understanding.
    *   **Limited LLM Coverage:** Although the coverage of LLMs is relatively good, it could be further improved by including a few more open-source LLMs.
    *   **Complexity of Thalia Type:** While the ThaliaType is novel, there may be some inherent biases in how code is generated using Thalia that could affect the findings, although mitigated by comparing to SnR performance.
*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research in this area by:

    *   Raising awareness of the data leakage problem.
    *   Encouraging the development and use of unseen datasets for LLM evaluation.
    *   Promoting more rigorous evaluation methodologies.
    *   Guiding the design of more robust LLMs that are less susceptible to overfitting.
*   **Rigorous rationale for score**: the paper is important because it highlights an issue that is potentially skewing the results of research in the area of LLMs. The novel methodology it proposes to deal with this issue are likely to be adopted by researchers in the future.

**Score: 8**

**Justification:** The paper makes a valuable and timely contribution by highlighting and empirically demonstrating the impact of data leakage on LLM evaluation. The novel ThaliaType dataset and the rigorous methodology provide a solid foundation for future research. While there are some limitations in the scope of transformations and LLM coverage, the core message and the overall quality of the work justify a high score. It is not a "10" because the techniques used, while effectively adapted, were not entirely new, and there's always room for expanding the experimental setup and analysis. However, it's a highly influential piece of work within its domain. The paper provides a useful contribution to LLM research by pointing out an area of concern and providing a path forward to overcome it.

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 8/10

### PokéChamp: an Expert-level Minimax Language Agent
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04094v1)
- **Authors**: Seth Karten, Andy Luu Nguyen, Chi Jin
- **Abstract**: We introduce Pok\'eChamp, a minimax agent powered by Large Language Models (LLMs) for Pok\'emon battles. Built on a general framework for two-player competitive games, Pok\'eChamp leverages the generalist capabilities of LLMs to enhance minimax tree search. Specifically, LLMs replace three key modules: (1) player action sampling, (2) opponent modeling, and (3) value function estimation, enabling the agent to effectively utilize gameplay history and human knowledge to reduce the search space and address partial observability. Notably, our framework requires no additional LLM training. We evaluate Pok\'eChamp in the popular Gen 9 OU format. When powered by GPT-4o, it achieves a win rate of 76% against the best existing LLM-based bot and 84% against the strongest rule-based bot, demonstrating its superior performance. Even with an open-source 8-billion-parameter Llama 3.1 model, Pok\'eChamp consistently outperforms the previous best LLM-based bot, Pok\'ellmon powered by GPT-4o, with a 64% win rate. Pok\'eChamp attains a projected Elo of 1300-1500 on the Pok\'emon Showdown online ladder, placing it among the top 30%-10% of human players. In addition, this work compiles the largest real-player Pok\'emon battle dataset, featuring over 3 million games, including more than 500k high-Elo matches. Based on this dataset, we establish a series of battle benchmarks and puzzles to evaluate specific battling skills. We further provide key updates to the local game engine. We hope this work fosters further research that leverage Pok\'emon battle as benchmark to integrate LLM technologies with game-theoretic algorithms addressing general multiagent problems. Videos, code, and dataset available at https://sites.google.com/view/pokechamp-llm.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and rigorous evaluation of the "PokéChamp: an Expert-level Minimax Language Agent" paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces PokéChamp, a novel AI agent for Pokémon battles that leverages Large Language Models (LLMs) to enhance minimax tree search. The LLMs replace key modules traditionally requiring task-specific training: player action sampling, opponent modeling, and value function estimation. PokéChamp, powered by GPT-4o, demonstrates strong performance against existing LLM-based and rule-based bots in the Generation 9 OverUsed format. The agent achieves a win rate of 76% against the best LLM bot and 84% against the strongest rule-based bot. The authors also show promising results using an open-source Llama 3.1 model. Additionally, the paper contributes a large Pokémon battle dataset (3+ million games), battle benchmarks, and updates to the local game engine.

**Rigorous Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Novelty:** The core idea of replacing key components of minimax tree search with LLMs *without* requiring additional LLM training is a significant contribution. Existing LLM-based game agents often rely on extensive task-specific finetuning. PokéChamp's approach highlights the generalist capabilities of LLMs. The integration is also fairly novel, going beyond simply using an LLM to choose actions and weaving it into a structured search process.
    *   **Performance:** The empirical results are compelling. Achieving expert-level play (top 30-10% on the online ladder) and outperforming state-of-the-art baselines is a strong validation of the approach. The use of both closed-source (GPT-4o) and open-source (Llama 3.1) models strengthens the generalizability of the findings.
    *   **Resources:** The release of a large, high-quality Pokémon battle dataset is a valuable resource for the research community. The battle benchmarks and engine updates also contribute to the reproducibility and advancement of research in this area.
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The authors clearly articulate the challenges of Pokémon battles as a complex, partially observable game and justify their approach.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Generalizability Beyond Pokémon:** While the framework *could* theoretically be adapted to other two-player competitive games, the specific design choices and prompts are highly tailored to Pokémon. The paper doesn't demonstrate a seamless transition to another domain. This limits its broader applicability.
    *   **Computational Cost:** The paper doesn't extensively discuss the computational cost of using LLMs within the minimax search. While the authors address the time constraint by limiting the search depth and relying on the one-step lookahead, practical deployment at scale would likely require further optimization and resource management. The paper only mentions that PokéChamp lost about one-third of games due to exceeding turn time limits, suggesting that this could be a more pressing issue than is acknowledged.
    *   **Black Box LLM:** While the black-box nature of the LLM allows for flexibility, it also makes it difficult to understand exactly *why* the agent is making certain decisions. It's hard to attribute performance to specific components of the LLM's knowledge or reasoning process.
    *   **Vulnerability to Adversarial Strategies:** The paper identifies specific weaknesses, such as stall tactics and excessive switching. These vulnerabilities suggest that the agent's opponent modeling and strategic planning are not yet fully robust, especially considering the human strategy which exploited switching limitations. While acknowledged, this limits the "expert-level" claim.
    *   **Static Opponent Modeling:** The static opponent modeling is also a source of potential strategic weakness. The ability to adapt *during* a game based on observed behavior would likely increase performance, but is not tackled in the paper.

*   **Significance:** The paper contributes to the growing field of LLM-enhanced game-playing agents. It offers a viable alternative to task-specific training, demonstrating that generalist knowledge from LLMs can be effectively integrated into game-theoretic algorithms. The limitations identified also provide valuable directions for future research.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper represents a significant advance in LLM-based game-playing agents, particularly in a challenging, partially observable domain. The empirical results are strong, and the resources provided are valuable to the community. However, the limited generalizability, computational cost considerations, black-box nature of the LLM, and identified vulnerabilities prevent it from being a truly groundbreaking contribution. The authors did not present any convincing evidence the work could be applied to different competitive games, and the reliance on domain-specific information weakens the findings.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Chart-HQA: A Benchmark for Hypothetical Question Answering in Charts
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04095v1)
- **Authors**: Xiangnan Chen, Yuancheng Fang, Qian Xiao, Juncheng Li, Jun Lin, Siliang Tang, Yi Yang, Yueting Zhuang
- **Abstract**: Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have garnered significant attention for their strong visual-semantic understanding. Most existing chart benchmarks evaluate MLLMs' ability to parse information from charts to answer questions.However, they overlook the inherent output biases of MLLMs, where models rely on their parametric memory to answer questions rather than genuinely understanding the chart content. To address this limitation, we introduce a novel Chart Hypothetical Question Answering (HQA) task, which imposes assumptions on the same question to compel models to engage in counterfactual reasoning based on the chart content. Furthermore, we introduce HAI, a human-AI interactive data synthesis approach that leverages the efficient text-editing capabilities of LLMs alongside human expert knowledge to generate diverse and high-quality HQA data at a low cost. Using HAI, we construct Chart-HQA, a challenging benchmark synthesized from publicly available data sources. Evaluation results on 18 MLLMs of varying model sizes reveal that current models face significant generalization challenges and exhibit imbalanced reasoning performance on the HQA task.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Chart-HQA: A Benchmark for Hypothetical Question Answering in Charts":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Chart-HQA, a novel benchmark designed to assess the true chart understanding capabilities of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs). The benchmark addresses the issue of MLLMs' tendency to rely on parametric memory rather than genuinely interpreting chart content, which existing benchmarks often overlook. Chart-HQA utilizes hypothetical question answering (HQA), where models must engage in counterfactual reasoning based on imposed assumptions, forcing them to interpret the visual content of charts. The paper also proposes HAI, a human-AI interactive data synthesis approach to create diverse and high-quality HQA data at low cost. Experiments on 18 MLLMs demonstrate that current models struggle with generalization and exhibit imbalanced reasoning performance on the HQA task.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper is **moderately novel**. The idea of using hypothetical question answering to probe deeper chart understanding in MLLMs is a valuable contribution. Current QA benchmarks mainly test simple information retrieval. Introducing counterfactual scenarios is a creative way to force models to reason beyond rote memorization and extraction. The proposed HAI data synthesis approach combines human expertise and LLM capabilities, providing a cost-effective alternative to fully manual data annotation. However, hypothetical QA, while relatively new in the context of charts, isn't an entirely novel concept in the broader QA literature. It has been explored in other domains.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance lies in highlighting the limitations of current MLLMs in truly understanding visual data, specifically charts. Chart-HQA provides a valuable tool for researchers to identify and address the output bias issue that plagues existing models. The benchmark has the potential to drive the development of more robust and reliable MLLMs for visual reasoning. The HAI method could have broader implications for data synthesis in other areas of visual-language research.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Well-defined problem:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of output bias in existing chart QA benchmarks.
    *   **Creative approach:** The hypothetical question answering task is a clever and effective way to elicit genuine chart understanding.
    *   **Practical data synthesis:** The HAI method is a practical approach for creating large, diverse datasets at a reasonable cost.
    *   **Comprehensive evaluation:** The paper evaluates a wide range of MLLMs, including specialized and generalist models.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Dataset scale:** While the paper mentions the dataset being challenging, the size of Chart-HQA (2173 questions) is relatively small compared to some other visual QA benchmarks. Expanding the dataset could provide more robust results and greater generalizability.
    *   **HAI complexity:** While effective, the HAI method's two-stage LLM-assisted process may not scale as easily as approaches that require less feedback/intervention.

*   **Justification for Score:** The paper's strengths outweigh its weaknesses. The novelty of the HQA task and the practicality of the HAI method, combined with the insightful evaluation of existing MLLMs, make it a worthwhile contribution to the field. However, the relatively small dataset size and moderate complexity of the proposed data generation method keep it from being a truly groundbreaking contribution.
    *   *Impact:* The work has the potential to shift the research focus from simple information extraction to deeper chart understanding, which can be valuable for applications like data visualization and analysis tools.
    *   *Rigor:* The experimental setup and analysis seem sound, but more data could make them even stronger.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Disparities in LLM Reasoning Accuracy and Explanations: A Case Study on African American English
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04099v1)
- **Authors**: Runtao Zhou, Guangya Wan, Saadia Gabriel, Sheng Li, Alexander J Gates, Maarten Sap, Thomas Hartvigsen
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable capabilities in reasoning tasks, leading to their widespread deployment. However, recent studies have highlighted concerning biases in these models, particularly in their handling of dialectal variations like African American English (AAE). In this work, we systematically investigate dialectal disparities in LLM reasoning tasks. We develop an experimental framework comparing LLM performance given Standard American English (SAE) and AAE prompts, combining LLM-based dialect conversion with established linguistic analyses. We find that LLMs consistently produce less accurate responses and simpler reasoning chains and explanations for AAE inputs compared to equivalent SAE questions, with disparities most pronounced in social science and humanities domains. These findings highlight systematic differences in how LLMs process and reason about different language varieties, raising important questions about the development and deployment of these systems in our multilingual and multidialectal world. Our code repository is publicly available at https://github.com/Runtaozhou/dialect_bias_eval.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates dialectal disparities in Large Language Model (LLM) reasoning, focusing on African American English (AAE) versus Standard American English (SAE). It develops an experimental framework combining LLM-based dialect conversion with established linguistic analysis to compare LLM performance on SAE and AAE prompts. The study reveals that LLMs consistently produce less accurate responses and simpler reasoning chains for AAE inputs, particularly in social science and humanities. The authors further analyze explanation quality in terms of readability and psychological expressions, revealing systematic differences. The paper also explores preliminary mitigation strategies using prompt engineering techniques.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The paper's novelty lies in its systematic and multi-faceted investigation of dialectal bias in LLM *reasoning*. While previous work has examined bias in tasks like toxicity detection or text generation, this study digs deeper into the reasoning processes themselves, examining accuracy, explanation complexity, and linguistic markers. The focus on explanation quality is particularly valuable, as it goes beyond simple accuracy to consider the socio-cognitive dimensions of LLM outputs. The integration of LLM-based dialect conversion with linguistic analysis is also a valuable methodological contribution.
*   **Significance:** The findings have important implications for the equitable development and deployment of LLMs, especially in high-stakes domains like education and healthcare. If LLMs provide less accurate or less sophisticated reasoning and explanations for AAE speakers, this could reinforce existing systemic biases. The study underscores the need for more careful consideration of dialectal variations in LLM training and evaluation.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   The experimental framework is well-designed and comprehensive, combining automated dialect conversion with established linguistic analysis and human evaluation.
    *   The study considers multiple dimensions of reasoning quality, including accuracy, readability, psychological expressions, and consistency.
    *   The analysis is thorough, examining a range of LLMs and different types of reasoning tasks.
    *   The paper identifies preliminary mitigation strategies, offering practical solutions for reducing dialectal disparities.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The dialect conversion process, while improved, still relies on LLMs, which may introduce some degree of artificiality or simplification. While authors demonstrate high semantic equivalence in reverting the AAE questions to SAE, potential semantic loss is still a concern.
    *   The sample size of human evaluations (100 sentences per metric for dialect conversion validation) might be a limiting factor for statistical power.
    *   The mitigation strategies explored are preliminary, and further research is needed to develop more effective and robust debiasing techniques.
    *   The study mainly focuses on written text and may not fully capture the nuances of spoken AAE, including prosodic features.
*   **Potential Impact:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence the field of NLP by raising awareness of dialectal biases in LLM reasoning and providing a framework for future research. It could also inform the development of more equitable and inclusive LLMs for diverse populations.

**Justification of Score:**

This is a well-executed study that makes a valuable contribution to our understanding of dialectal bias in LLMs. The multi-dimensional analysis of reasoning quality, the consideration of explanation sophistication, and the exploration of preliminary mitigation strategies are all significant strengths. While there are some limitations related to dialect conversion and sample size, the overall rigor and potential impact of the work justify a high score. This research should be useful to practitioners aiming to reduce biases in LLMs.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### LLMs Can Generate a Better Answer by Aggregating Their Own Responses
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04104v1)
- **Authors**: Zichong Li, Xinyu Feng, Yuheng Cai, Zixuan Zhang, Tianyi Liu, Chen Liang, Weizhu Chen, Haoyu Wang, Tuo Zhao
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown remarkable capabilities across tasks, yet they often require additional prompting techniques when facing complex problems. While approaches like self-correction and response selection have emerged as popular solutions, recent studies have shown these methods perform poorly when relying on the LLM itself to provide feedback or selection criteria. We argue this limitation stems from the fact that common LLM post-training procedures lack explicit supervision for discriminative judgment tasks. In this paper, we propose Generative Self-Aggregation (GSA), a novel prompting method that improves answer quality without requiring the model's discriminative capabilities. GSA first samples multiple diverse responses from the LLM, then aggregates them to obtain an improved solution. Unlike previous approaches, our method does not require the LLM to correct errors or compare response quality; instead, it leverages the model's generative abilities to synthesize a new response based on the context of multiple samples. While GSA shares similarities with the self-consistency (SC) approach for response aggregation, SC requires specific verifiable tokens to enable majority voting. In contrast, our approach is more general and can be applied to open-ended tasks. Empirical evaluation demonstrates that GSA effectively improves response quality across various tasks, including mathematical reasoning, knowledge-based problems, and open-ended generation tasks such as code synthesis and conversational responses.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Generative Self-Aggregation (GSA), a novel prompting method designed to enhance the performance of Large Language Models (LLMs) without relying on their discriminative capabilities.  GSA operates by first generating multiple diverse responses to a given prompt, and then leveraging these responses as context to prompt the model to synthesize an improved solution. This method differs from approaches like self-correction or choose-from-N, which often rely on the LLM's ability to judge and select the best response – an area where LLMs often underperform due to lack of explicit training.  GSA leverages the LLM's inherent generative abilities to learn from the diverse set of generated solutions and produce a more refined answer.  The authors demonstrate GSA's effectiveness across a range of tasks including mathematical reasoning, knowledge-based problems, and open-ended generation tasks like code synthesis and conversational responses. The experiments show that GSA outperforms self-correction and choose-from-N methods, and achieves comparable or better performance than self-consistency where applicable, without requiring specialized training or external feedback.

**Rigorous and Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper introduces a novel prompting strategy that directly addresses a key limitation of current LLMs: their weak discriminative abilities.  The idea of using multiple generated responses as context for synthesizing a better answer is a clever departure from methods that rely on selecting among generated outputs. While self-consistency also aggregates multiple responses, GSA takes a more holistic approach by leveraging the reasoning processes within the various generated responses to inform the generative step. The core innovation lies in framing the problem as one of generative synthesis rather than discriminative selection.

*   **Significance:** GSA has the potential to significantly improve LLM performance across a wide array of tasks, especially in scenarios where external feedback or specialized training data are not readily available. The method's generality and simplicity are strengths. The empirical results across several datasets and model scales convincingly demonstrate GSA's effectiveness.  A particularly important aspect is the improvement in open-ended generation tasks, where self-consistency is not directly applicable. The ablation studies, specifically those examining the number of responses and sampling temperatures, are valuable for understanding the method's behavior. The analysis of likelihood distributions provides further insight into why GSA works, lending empirical support to the core hypothesis.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the limitations of existing LLM prompting techniques.
    *   **Novel Approach:**  GSA offers a novel and effective solution.
    *   **Strong Empirical Validation:** Extensive experiments across various tasks and model sizes support the claims.
    *   **Insightful Analysis:** The paper provides insightful analysis of GSA's behavior through ablation studies and likelihood distribution analysis.
    *   **Generality:** GSA is widely applicable and doesn't depend on specific task characteristics or external resources.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Computational Cost:** While GSA doesn't require training, it does increase inference cost due to the need to generate multiple responses. However, this trade-off is often worthwhile given the substantial performance gains.
    *   **Parameter Tuning:** While the method is simple, the temperature and the optimal number of responses could be task-dependent, requiring some tuning.
    *   **Comparison to Best-of-N:** The paper doesn't always show the "Best-of-N (Oracle)" performance, especially in ablation studies. Including this could have provided more context on the potential improvement range for each task.

*   **Potential Influence:**  GSA can influence future research in several ways:

    *   **Prompt Engineering:**  GSA can inspire the development of more sophisticated prompting techniques that leverage the generative capabilities of LLMs.
    *   **LLM Training:** The GSA framework demonstrates the importance of training LLMs not only for generative tasks, but also for effectively combining information from multiple sources.
    *   **Applications:** GSA can be directly applied to improve the performance of LLMs in various applications, especially in areas requiring complex reasoning or open-ended generation.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and effective prompting technique that addresses a key limitation in LLMs.  The empirical results are compelling, and the analysis provides valuable insights into the method's behavior. While there are some limitations in terms of increased computational cost and parameter tuning, the potential impact of GSA on the field of LLMs is significant. It provides a simple yet powerful way to improve LLM performance without additional training or external feedback.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### InterChat: Enhancing Generative Visual Analytics using Multimodal Interactions
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04110v1)
- **Authors**: Juntong Chen, Jiang Wu, Jiajing Guo, Vikram Mohanty, Xueming Li, Jorge Piazentin Ono, Wenbin He, Liu Ren, Dongyu Liu
- **Abstract**: The rise of Large Language Models (LLMs) and generative visual analytics systems has transformed data-driven insights, yet significant challenges persist in accurately interpreting users' analytical and interaction intents. While language inputs offer flexibility, they often lack precision, making the expression of complex intents inefficient, error-prone, and time-intensive. To address these limitations, we investigate the design space of multimodal interactions for generative visual analytics through a literature review and pilot brainstorming sessions. Building on these insights, we introduce a highly extensible workflow that integrates multiple LLM agents for intent inference and visualization generation. We develop InterChat, a generative visual analytics system that combines direct manipulation of visual elements with natural language inputs. This integration enables precise intent communication and supports progressive, visually driven exploratory data analyses. By employing effective prompt engineering, and contextual interaction linking, alongside intuitive visualization and interaction designs, InterChat bridges the gap between user interactions and LLM-driven visualizations, enhancing both interpretability and usability. Extensive evaluations, including two usage scenarios, a user study, and expert feedback, demonstrate the effectiveness of InterChat. Results show significant improvements in the accuracy and efficiency of handling complex visual analytics tasks, highlighting the potential of multimodal interactions to redefine user engagement and analytical depth in generative visual analytics.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**
