    *   It is also unclear why a purely logistic regression model is chosen instead of a more advanced model such as causal inference or structural equation modeling that might be able to explain the complex relationship among these variables.
    *   The limited exploration of the specific prompts and LLM models used (which are not necessarily the top performing open source or proprietary models available) needs to be expanded.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence future research by encouraging more quantitative studies of creativity in hackathons and other real-world settings. The use of LLMs for creativity assessment could inspire further work in this area, leading to more sophisticated methods for evaluating creative outcomes.

**Justification for the Score:**

This paper presents an important contribution to the field by addressing a practical and significant gap: the lack of large-scale quantitative analyses of creativity in hackathons. However, the operationalization of creativity, and the lack of robustness of the AI judge element limits its overall impact. The paper demonstrates methodological limitations including the lack of robustness of its baseline human judges to justify a higher score.

**Score: 6.5**
- **Classification**: cs.HC
- **Score**: 6/10

### MathMistake Checker: A Comprehensive Demonstration for Step-by-Step Math Problem Mistake Finding by Prompt-Guided LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04291v1)
- **Authors**: Tianyang Zhang, Zhuoxuan Jiang, Haotian Zhang, Lin Lin, Shaohua Zhang
- **Abstract**: We propose a novel system, MathMistake Checker, designed to automate step-by-step mistake finding in mathematical problems with lengthy answers through a two-stage process. The system aims to simplify grading, increase efficiency, and enhance learning experiences from a pedagogical perspective. It integrates advanced technologies, including computer vision and the chain-of-thought capabilities of the latest large language models (LLMs). Our system supports open-ended grading without reference answers and promotes personalized learning by providing targeted feedback. We demonstrate its effectiveness across various types of math problems, such as calculation and word problems.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MathMistake Checker, a two-stage system designed to automate the grading of step-by-step mathematical problem solutions, particularly for handwritten answers. The system integrates Optical Character Recognition (OCR) and Large Language Models (LLMs). Stage 1 (OCR Module) converts image-based student submissions into textual data via a pipeline involving text line segmentation, specialized text recognition (handwritten and printed), document layout analysis, and order detection. Stage 2 (Grading Module) uses an LLM (via Pedagogical Chain-of-Thought prompting) to regenerate/predict the expected next step, compare it with the student's actual response, and evaluate/comment on the student's reasoning, providing targeted feedback. The paper demonstrates the system's effectiveness on calculation and word problems.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper addresses a relevant and important problem: automating the laborious task of grading step-by-step math solutions, which has significant pedagogical implications.
    *   The system leverages a well-structured two-stage approach that combines OCR and LLMs, allowing for both the interpretation of visual inputs and the evaluation of logical reasoning.
    *   The modular design (OCR and Grading Modules) enhances interpretability and allows for independent development and refinement of each module.
    *   The use of Pedagogical Chain-of-Thought prompting demonstrates a thoughtful attempt to emulate human teacher feedback.
    *   The paper provides a good overview of the system's architecture and workflow.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The paper relies heavily on existing technologies (OCR pipelines and LLMs). The novelty of *integrating* these components isn't clearly demonstrated, nor is the customization of these components. The paper describes fairly standard implementations of OCR components.
    *   The evaluation is limited. The paper presents a demo interface but lacks quantitative results comparing the system's performance against human graders or other automated systems. It's difficult to assess the accuracy, reliability, and usefulness of the generated feedback.
    *   The paper doesn't deeply explore the limitations of the system. What types of mathematical problems are challenging for the current implementation? What are the error rates of the OCR module, and how do these errors affect the grading module?  What are the computational costs associated with running the LLM?
    *   The "pedagogical significance" is asserted but not convincingly validated. A user study (even a small one) with teachers or students would be much more impactful.
    *   The discussion of LLM prompt strategies feels cursory. The impact of different prompt designs on feedback quality isn't explored.
    *   The claim of supporting "open-ended grading without reference answers" is only partially true.  The system still relies on LLMs to generate "correct" answers in each step, which acts as an implicit reference answer. The true value of the system lies in its ability to analyze and provide feedback even when the students follow a different correct approach.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**
    While the integration of OCR and LLMs for math grading is a logical next step in the field, the paper doesn't present significant breakthroughs or demonstrate substantial improvements over existing approaches. The true potential lies in the pedagogical benefits, but this is not well explored. The technical components themselves are fairly standard.

*   **Potential Influence:**
    The paper could inspire further research in automating math grading, especially in exploring more sophisticated LLM prompting strategies and incorporating more robust error correction mechanisms. However, without more rigorous evaluation, its immediate impact on the field will likely be limited.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper addresses a worthwhile problem with a reasonable approach, but it falls short in demonstrating significant novelty or presenting convincing evidence of its effectiveness. The lack of quantitative evaluation, limited exploration of limitations, and reliance on existing technologies undermine its contribution. While the modular design and use of Ped-CoT prompting are positives, they don't elevate the paper to a truly impactful contribution.  The lack of in-depth analysis prevents a higher score.

Score: 5

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 5/10

### Mapping AI Benchmark Data to Quantitative Risk Estimates Through Expert Elicitation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04299v1)
- **Authors**: Malcolm Murray, Henry Papadatos, Otter Quarks, Pierre-François Gimenez, Simeon Campos
- **Abstract**: The literature and multiple experts point to many potential risks from large language models (LLMs), but there are still very few direct measurements of the actual harms posed. AI risk assessment has so far focused on measuring the models' capabilities, but the capabilities of models are only indicators of risk, not measures of risk. Better modeling and quantification of AI risk scenarios can help bridge this disconnect and link the capabilities of LLMs to tangible real-world harm. This paper makes an early contribution to this field by demonstrating how existing AI benchmarks can be used to facilitate the creation of risk estimates. We describe the results of a pilot study in which experts use information from Cybench, an AI benchmark, to generate probability estimates. We show that the methodology seems promising for this purpose, while noting improvements that can be made to further strengthen its application in quantitative AI risk assessment.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores how AI benchmark data, specifically from the Cybench cybersecurity benchmark, can be mapped to quantitative risk estimates for AI-enabled cyberattacks through expert elicitation. The authors conducted a pilot study where cybersecurity experts used Cybench results to estimate the probability of successful malware development given access to LLMs.  The study reveals a divergence in expert opinions, suggests that current LLMs provide a slight cyber uplift, and that LLMs that saturate Cybench would provide meaningful assistance to cyber attackers. The authors advocate for more detailed risk models and evaluations that directly measure capabilities relevant to the steps of these models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's central idea – linking AI benchmark data to quantitative risk estimates via expert elicitation – represents a valuable contribution to the nascent field of AI risk assessment. While both AI risk assessment and AI benchmarks are areas of research, very little has connected them, particularly with quantitative methods and expert elicitation. This helps address the gap of only measuring capabilities in isolation without linking those capabilities to concrete real-world harms.
*   **Significance:** The work is significant because it attempts to move beyond simply measuring AI capabilities to assessing their potential real-world impact. By providing a framework that translates benchmark scores into concrete risk estimates, the paper can inform decision-making and guide the development of more operationally relevant AI benchmarks. The work makes the abstract AI capabilities concrete and ties them to possible negative societal consequences.
*   **Strengths:** The paper’s primary strength lies in demonstrating a methodology for bridging the gap between AI benchmark data and quantitative risk assessments. The use of the Cybench benchmark provides a practical context for the expert elicitation process, making it more tangible and actionable.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   *Small Sample Size:* The small number of experts participating in the elicitation process limits the generalizability of the findings.
    *   *Upward Bias:* Potential upward bias resulting from how the tasks were presented to the experts.
    *   *Indirect Measurement:* The study relies on expert interpretations of CTF task performance and its relationship to real-world malware development capabilities, introducing uncertainty.
    *   *Limited Scope:* The study focuses on a single step in a cyber risk model, successful malware development and deployment, which could limit the broader applicability of the methodology.
*   **Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence future research on AI risk assessment by highlighting the importance of quantitative methods and the value of linking benchmark data to real-world harms. The approach could be adopted and refined by regulators, policymakers, and AI developers seeking to ground AI regulation in empirical data and better manage the risks associated with AI systems. The paper makes a call for clearer measurement and transparency, tying it directly to AI regulation and societal impact.

*Rigorous Rationale:* The paper presents a novel approach and a preliminary investigation that ties abstract performance metrics of AI systems to real world harmful consequences by eliciting expert opinions. Although the sample size and experimental conditions are limited, the authors acknowledge that limitations and provide suggestions for further research. With a larger sample size and more robust expert elicitation methods, this would be a major paper. Because of the strong promise of the method and the experimental setup, the paper gets a reasonable rating.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 6/10

### Malware Detection at the Edge with Lightweight LLMs: A Performance Evaluation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04302v1)
- **Authors**: Christian Rondanini, Barbara Carminati, Elena Ferrari, Antonio Gaudiano, Ashish Kundu
- **Abstract**: The rapid evolution of malware attacks calls for the development of innovative detection methods, especially in resource-constrained edge computing. Traditional detection techniques struggle to keep up with modern malware's sophistication and adaptability, prompting a shift towards advanced methodologies like those leveraging Large Language Models (LLMs) for enhanced malware detection. However, deploying LLMs for malware detection directly at edge devices raises several challenges, including ensuring accuracy in constrained environments and addressing edge devices' energy and computational limits. To tackle these challenges, this paper proposes an architecture leveraging lightweight LLMs' strengths while addressing limitations like reduced accuracy and insufficient computational power. To evaluate the effectiveness of the proposed lightweight LLM-based approach for edge computing, we perform an extensive experimental evaluation using several state-of-the-art lightweight LLMs. We test them with several publicly available datasets specifically designed for edge and IoT scenarios and different edge nodes with varying computational power and characteristics.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper on malware detection at the edge using lightweight LLMs:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the feasibility and effectiveness of deploying lightweight Large Language Models (LLMs) on resource-constrained edge devices for malware detection.  It addresses the challenges of adapting LLMs, which are typically computationally expensive, to the limited capabilities of edge devices. The authors propose an architecture that focuses on fine-tuning small language models (SLMs) to specialize in specific malware types. They conduct an extensive experimental evaluation using several state-of-the-art SLMs (DistilBERT, TinyBERT, Llama-3.2 1B, DistilGPT-2, and TinyT5) and publicly available datasets designed for edge and IoT scenarios (Edge-IIoTset, X-IIoTID, TON-IoT, and CIC IoT Dataset 2023).  The evaluation considers accuracy, adaptability, and feasibility, including on-device performance measurements on Raspberry Pi and Jetson Nano. The experiments assess zero-shot performance, fine-tuning benefits (few-shot and complete datasets), and cross-dataset validation. The results indicate that fine-tuning SLMs is crucial for achieving acceptable accuracy and that certain models (TinyBERT and TinyT5) strike a good balance between performance and resource usage, making them promising candidates for edge deployment.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Experimental Evaluation:** The paper's main strength lies in its thorough experimental evaluation. It considers multiple models, datasets, scenarios (zero-shot, fine-tuning, cross-validation), and edge devices. The breadth of the experiments offers a well-rounded understanding of the problem.
    *   **Relevant Problem:** Malware detection at the edge is an important and timely research area. As IoT devices proliferate, securing them with local detection capabilities is crucial.
    *   **Practical Focus:** The paper not only evaluates the performance of LLMs but also considers the practical aspects of deploying them on real edge devices. The on-device performance measurements are particularly valuable.
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-structured and clearly presents the experimental setup, results, and analysis. The inclusion of tables summarizing key metrics is helpful.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Novelty in Approach:** The idea of using LLMs for malware detection, even at the edge, isn't entirely novel.  Many existing papers have used LLM-based models, although this paper offers a comprehensive comparison of LLM methods, and specifically is targeted to lightweight methods running at the edge, which increases novelty in the field.

    *   **Limited Discussion of Threat Model and Attack Scenarios:** While the datasets used cover various attacks, the paper could benefit from a more in-depth discussion of the specific types of malware targeted and the threat model assumed.  Understanding the characteristics of the malware (e.g., obfuscation techniques) would provide more context for the performance results.
    *   **Lack of Adaptability Evaluation Beyond Cross-Dataset Validation:** While cross-dataset validation provides some insight into adaptability, it doesn't fully capture the dynamic nature of malware. A more realistic evaluation would involve testing the models against newly emerging malware samples or simulated evolving attack scenarios.
    *   **Dependency on Existing Datasets:** The evaluation heavily relies on publicly available datasets, which might have limitations in terms of diversity and realism.  The results may not generalize perfectly to real-world edge environments with different traffic patterns.
    *   **Limited exploration of adversarial attacks**: There are a number of papers on generating adversarial attacks on LLMs. This is important as LLMs could be subject to these attacks if proper steps are not taken.
    *   **Overclaims**: The claims in the paper regarding the comprehensive performance evaluation may be exaggerated. While it is a thorough evaluation, it does not explore the model behavior for adversarial attacks.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper contributes a useful performance benchmark for lightweight LLMs in the context of edge-based malware detection. The experimental results provide valuable insights for researchers and practitioners working in this area.
    *   The practical focus on on-device performance highlights the challenges and opportunities of deploying LLMs on resource-constrained devices.
    *   The paper can serve as a starting point for further research on optimizing LLMs for edge-based security applications.

**Justification for Score:**

Despite its limitations, the paper's strength is its thorough experimental evaluation and practical considerations. The breadth and depth of the experiments, considering multiple models, datasets, scenarios, and edge devices, make this a useful contribution to the field. It has useful information regarding the feasibility of running LLMs in various scenarios.

However, the paper loses points due to its limited novelty in approach and lack of a strong novelty factor in the experimental setup. More discussion on threat model and attack scenarios will greatly improve the work, and a wider range of experiments to take into account the dynamic nature of malware would also be beneficial. Due to the above discussion, I cannot assign a very high score.

**Score: 6**
- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 6/10

### Solving Word-Sense Disambiguation and Word-Sense Induction with Dictionary Examples
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04328v1)
- **Authors**: Tadej Škvorc, Marko Robnik-Šikonja
- **Abstract**: Many less-resourced languages struggle with a lack of large, task-specific datasets that are required for solving relevant tasks with modern transformer-based large language models (LLMs). On the other hand, many linguistic resources, such as dictionaries, are rarely used in this context despite their large information contents. We show how LLMs can be used to extend existing language resources in less-resourced languages for two important tasks: word-sense disambiguation (WSD) and word-sense induction (WSI). We approach the two tasks through the related but much more accessible word-in-context (WiC) task where, given a pair of sentences and a target word, a classification model is tasked with predicting whether the sense of a given word differs between sentences. We demonstrate that a well-trained model for this task can distinguish between different word senses and can be adapted to solve the WSD and WSI tasks. The advantage of using the WiC task, instead of directly predicting senses, is that the WiC task does not need pre-constructed sense inventories with a sufficient number of examples for each sense, which are rarely available in less-resourced languages. We show that sentence pairs for the WiC task can be successfully generated from dictionary examples using LLMs. The resulting prediction models outperform existing models on WiC, WSD, and WSI tasks. We demonstrate our methodology on the Slovene language, where a monolingual dictionary is available, but word-sense resources are tiny.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of limited task-specific datasets for NLP tasks in less-resourced languages. It proposes a novel methodology that leverages dictionaries, which are more readily available, and large language models (LLMs) to generate training data for word-sense disambiguation (WSD) and word-sense induction (WSI). The approach involves using dictionary definitions and examples to create datasets for the word-in-context (WiC) task.  A WiC model is trained on this generated data and then adapted to solve WSD and WSI tasks through pairwise comparison of sentences.  The methodology is demonstrated on the Slovene language, where a monolingual dictionary exists but word-sense resources are scarce. The authors show that the generated data improves performance on WiC, WSD, and WSI tasks compared to using unextended dictionary examples.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in the specific combination of techniques and its application to less-resourced languages. While using LLMs for data augmentation and WiC for WSD/WSI is not entirely new, the systematic pipeline leveraging dictionary definitions to generate WiC training data and its careful adaptation for WSD/WSI in a low-resource setting offers a unique contribution. The explicit focus on using readily available linguistic resources (dictionaries) to overcome data scarcity is a significant aspect of the novelty.

*   **Significance:** The paper makes a practically significant contribution by providing a viable method for creating training data for WSD/WSI in less-resourced languages. This addresses a major bottleneck in NLP research and development for these languages, where lack of data is a major impediment. The method's reliance on dictionaries, a common linguistic resource, makes it widely applicable. The demonstrated improvements in WSD/WSI performance, even with automatically generated data, are promising.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Practicality:** The reliance on dictionaries makes the approach highly practical for less-resourced languages.
    *   **Clear Methodology:** The paper clearly explains the data generation pipeline, WiC model training, and adaptation to WSD/WSI.
    *   **Empirical Evaluation:** The paper provides a comprehensive empirical evaluation, including manual analysis of generated sentences and quantitative evaluation on WiC, WSD, and WSI tasks.
    *   **Focus on OOV words:** They provide results for the OOV setting which is very practical.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope of Manual Evaluation:** While the manual evaluation of generated sentences provides insights into the quality of the generated data, it is based on a relatively small sample size (200 sentences).
    *   **Dependency on LLM Quality:** The quality of the generated data depends heavily on the capabilities of the LLM used (GPT-3.5 in this case). Future improvements in LLMs could further enhance the effectiveness of this approach.
    *   **Potential for Bias:** The LLM might introduce biases present in its training data, which could affect the quality and fairness of the generated datasets. This aspect is not explicitly addressed in the paper.
    *   **Generalizability:** Though the authors claim generalizability due to the commonality of dictionaries, the experiments are limited to Slovene.  Further evaluation across other less-resourced languages is necessary to validate the general applicability.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper's approach could inspire further research on leveraging existing linguistic resources for data augmentation in low-resource NLP. It could also influence the development of more sophisticated techniques for generating training data using LLMs, with a specific focus on controlling biases and ensuring quality. The WiC-based approach could become a standard technique for WSD/WSI in data-scarce environments.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper has some limitations, its novelty in combining techniques, practical relevance for less-resourced languages, and promising empirical results justify a good score. The proposed method addresses a significant problem in the field and provides a viable solution. The reliance on widely available linguistic resources makes the approach highly adaptable. The comprehensive empirical evaluation strengthens the paper's claims. I'm reducing the score due to experimentations only in Slovene and possible LLM biases.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### The Challenge of Identifying the Origin of Black-Box Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04332v1)
- **Authors**: Ziqing Yang, Yixin Wu, Yun Shen, Wei Dai, Michael Backes, Yang Zhang
- **Abstract**: The tremendous commercial potential of large language models (LLMs) has heightened concerns about their unauthorized use. Third parties can customize LLMs through fine-tuning and offer only black-box API access, effectively concealing unauthorized usage and complicating external auditing processes. This practice not only exacerbates unfair competition, but also violates licensing agreements. In response, identifying the origin of black-box LLMs is an intrinsic solution to this issue. In this paper, we first reveal the limitations of state-of-the-art passive and proactive identification methods with experiments on 30 LLMs and two real-world black-box APIs. Then, we propose the proactive technique, PlugAE, which optimizes adversarial token embeddings in a continuous space and proactively plugs them into the LLM for tracing and identification. The experiments show that PlugAE can achieve substantial improvement in identifying fine-tuned derivatives. We further advocate for legal frameworks and regulations to better address the challenges posed by the unauthorized use of LLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of identifying the origin of black-box large language models (LLMs), particularly when these models have been fine-tuned and offered as API services without proper attribution, violating licensing agreements. The authors first analyze limitations of existing passive and proactive LLM identification methods, demonstrating their unreliability with experiments on 30 LLMs and two APIs. They then propose a novel proactive technique called PlugAE, which optimizes adversarial token embeddings and plugs them into the LLM without modifying the model's core weights, preserving its utility. Experimental results suggest PlugAE significantly improves the identification of fine-tuned derivatives, offering a more robust solution. The paper concludes by advocating for legal frameworks and regulations to better address the unauthorized use of LLMs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in the PlugAE technique. Existing AE-based methods rely on discrete optimization of adversarial texts, which are inherently limited. PlugAE shifts the focus to continuous token embedding optimization, offering a more efficient and effective approach. Also, the method's ability to inject "watermarks" without modifying model weights is a significant benefit over existing fine-tuning-based watermarking methods, preserving utility and mitigating the risk of the watermark being lost during further customization. Formalization of existing AE-based passive identification methods under a unified loss and the subsequent theoretical analysis of their limitations add to the paper's novel aspects.

*   **Significance:** The problem addressed is highly significant. The increasing prevalence of LLMs and their commercial potential necessitate mechanisms for ensuring proper attribution and preventing unauthorized use.  Violations of licensing agreements and the rise of unofficial channels distributing modified models are real threats to fair competition and innovation. PlugAE represents a tangible contribution towards tackling this challenge. The paper's empirical evaluation, involving a substantial number of models and APIs, reinforces its practical relevance.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clearly defined problem statement and threat model.
    *   Comprehensive evaluation of existing methods, highlighting their limitations.
    *   Theoretically grounded approach to PlugAE, with formal analysis supporting its potential benefits.
    *   Extensive empirical validation with diverse LLMs and real-world APIs.
    *   Clear exposition and well-structured presentation.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The paper relies on certain assumptions, such as the injectivity of the embedding layer, that may not hold for all LLMs. While empirical evidence is provided, further investigation of these assumptions would strengthen the analysis.
    *   The experiments focus primarily on identifying derivatives obtained through fine-tuning. The robustness of PlugAE against more sophisticated obfuscation techniques, such as weight rearrangement or adversarial training against the watermark, is not fully explored.
    *   The counterexample analysis in results reveals a potential drawback of PlugAE that the weight deviation would have great impact.

*   **Potential Impact:** The PlugAE technique has the potential to become a valuable tool for LLM owners and regulators in combating unauthorized use and enforcing licensing agreements. The paper's findings could also inform the development of more robust and reliable LLM identification methods. It is a crucial step toward ensuring transparency and accountability in LLM development and deployment. The call for legal frameworks and regulations raises important questions about the ethical and legal implications of LLM technology.

**Justification of Score:**

Considering the factors above, I assign a score of 7.5. The paper demonstrates clear novelty through the PlugAE technique and addresses a highly significant problem with a comprehensive experimental setup. The theoretical analysis and clear exposition are also noteworthy strengths.  However, the robustness against advanced attacks and dependence on the weight of models could be further explored, and the assumptions could be strengthened with more investigation. The theoretical and practical aspects combine to make this paper a valuable contribution, though some limitations prevent a higher score.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 7/10

### In-depth Analysis of Graph-based RAG in a Unified Framework
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04338v1)
- **Authors**: Yingli Zhou, Yaodong Su, Youran Sun, Shu Wang, Taotao Wang, Runyuan He, Yongwei Zhang, Sicong Liang, Xilin Liu, Yuchi Ma, Yixiang Fang
- **Abstract**: Graph-based Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) has proven effective in integrating external knowledge into large language models (LLMs), improving their factual accuracy, adaptability, interpretability, and trustworthiness. A number of graph-based RAG methods have been proposed in the literature. However, these methods have not been systematically and comprehensively compared under the same experimental settings. In this paper, we first summarize a unified framework to incorporate all graph-based RAG methods from a high-level perspective. We then extensively compare representative graph-based RAG methods over a range of questing-answering (QA) datasets -- from specific questions to abstract questions -- and examine the effectiveness of all methods, providing a thorough analysis of graph-based RAG approaches. As a byproduct of our experimental analysis, we are also able to identify new variants of the graph-based RAG methods over specific QA and abstract QA tasks respectively, by combining existing techniques, which outperform the state-of-the-art methods. Finally, based on these findings, we offer promising research opportunities. We believe that a deeper understanding of the behavior of existing methods can provide new valuable insights for future research.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper presents an in-depth analysis of graph-based Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) methods. The authors propose a unified framework to incorporate various graph-based RAG approaches and then conduct extensive comparative experiments across a range of question-answering datasets, spanning specific and abstract question types.  The paper identifies new variants of graph-based RAG methods by combining existing techniques, achieving state-of-the-art performance. Finally, the paper offers a discussion of promising research opportunities and lessons learned. The authors claim a comprehensive evaluation compared to prior art, and a publicly available testbed for graph-based RAG is provided.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper offers several novel contributions. The primary novelty lies in the *unified framework* for analyzing and categorizing graph-based RAG methods. This framework provides a structured way to understand and compare different approaches, moving beyond ad-hoc comparisons.  The identification of new RAG variants through combining existing techniques (e.g. VGraphRAG) and the subsequent experimental validation also demonstrates novelty. The analysis of different RAG methods applied to *abstract* QA is also a solid contribution, as this area has received less attention than fact-based QA.

* **Significance:**  The significance of this work stems from its potential to accelerate research and development in the field of RAG. A unified framework and a readily available testbed provide a common ground for researchers to build upon. The comprehensive experimental results provide valuable insights into the strengths and weaknesses of different approaches, enabling informed design decisions for future RAG systems. The performance gains observed by the newly proposed VGraphRAG method further solidify the significance. The identification of open research questions points toward important future directions for the community. The focus on *abstract* QA is highly relevant, addressing a more complex, open-ended aspect of question answering where LLMs often struggle.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper provides a comprehensive experimental evaluation comparing a large number of graph-based RAG methods across a variety of datasets.
    * **Unified Framework:** The proposed framework is a valuable contribution, offering a structured way to analyze and compare different methods.
    * **Novel Variants:**  The creation and evaluation of new RAG variants demonstrating SOTA performance.
    * **Publicly Available Testbed:** The availability of the code and data facilitates reproducibility and further research.
    * **Detailed Analysis:** The analysis delves into different aspects of the methods, such as token cost, graph size, and the impact of different components.
    * **Well-organized and clearly written:** The paper is easy to follow and understand.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Resource limitations:** The authors acknowledge token length limitations, which may impact the robustness of certain conclusions. While they used a strong backbone model (Llama-3-70B), experimenting with even larger models (e.g., models beyond 100 billion parameters) would strengthen the findings.
    * **Prompt Sensitivity:** The study is limited by not conducting prompt ablation studies, instead relying on prompts from the original papers. This may introduce a bias in the results. The authors should mention and ideally implement Prompt Tuning Tools like DSPy in further revisions of the work.
    * **Datasets:** Even with its breadth, the dataset coverage might not fully represent the spectrum of real-world applications. More domain-specific or task-oriented datasets could provide further insights.
    * **Scalability Analysis:** While the paper explores token costs, a more thorough scalability analysis (e.g., how the methods perform with much larger datasets or knowledge graphs) would be beneficial.
   * **Limited novelty around algorithm modifications.** The core improvements hinge on clever combinations of existing tools, not fundamentally new algorithm design.

* **Potential Influence:** This work has the potential to influence future research in several ways:
    * Serving as a benchmark for evaluating new graph-based RAG methods.
    * Guiding the design of more effective RAG systems by highlighting the key factors influencing performance.
    * Encouraging research into more efficient and scalable graph construction and retrieval techniques.
    * Inspiring new combinations of existing techniques to create novel RAG variants.
    * Stimulating research in the area of abstract QA.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper offers a substantial contribution to the field of RAG by providing a much-needed systematic analysis and a unified framework. While not revolutionizing a single technique, the insights gained through rigorous experimentation, the identified best practices, and the publicly available resource are all valuable assets. While the authors are combining elements that exist already, their work provides a significant, valuable understanding of how these elements fit together to form a RAG system. The novelty of the *new* algorithm is limited, but the paper *does* provide the field with an important experimental investigation.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 7/10

### LEDiT: Your Length-Extrapolatable Diffusion Transformer without Positional Encoding
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04344v1)
- **Authors**: Shen Zhang, Yaning Tan, Siyuan Liang, Linze Li, Ge Wu, Yuhao Chen, Shuheng Li, Zhenyu Zhao, Caihua Chen, Jiajun Liang, Yao Tang
- **Abstract**: Diffusion transformers(DiTs) struggle to generate images at resolutions higher than their training resolutions. The primary obstacle is that the explicit positional encodings(PE), such as RoPE, need extrapolation which degrades performance when the inference resolution differs from training. In this paper, we propose a Length-Extrapolatable Diffusion Transformer(LEDiT), a simple yet powerful architecture to overcome this limitation. LEDiT needs no explicit PEs, thereby avoiding extrapolation. The key innovations of LEDiT are introducing causal attention to implicitly impart global positional information to tokens, while enhancing locality to precisely distinguish adjacent tokens. Experiments on 256x256 and 512x512 ImageNet show that LEDiT can scale the inference resolution to 512x512 and 1024x1024, respectively, while achieving better image quality compared to current state-of-the-art length extrapolation methods(NTK-aware, YaRN). Moreover, LEDiT achieves strong extrapolation performance with just 100K steps of fine-tuning on a pretrained DiT, demonstrating its potential for integration into existing text-to-image DiTs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces LEDiT (Length-Extrapolatable Diffusion Transformer), a diffusion transformer architecture designed to generate high-quality images at resolutions exceeding its training resolution. LEDiT eliminates the need for explicit positional encodings (PEs), a common bottleneck in existing diffusion transformers that struggle with length extrapolation due to PE degradation when the inference resolution differs from the training resolution.  The key innovations are the incorporation of causal attention to implicitly encode global positional information and the use of negligible-cost convolutions with a multi-dilation training strategy to enhance local fine-grained details. The authors demonstrate superior performance on ImageNet compared to state-of-the-art length extrapolation methods like NTK-aware scaling and YaRN, and also show it can be integrated into existing text-to-image DiTs with just 100K steps of fine-tuning.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

*   **Elimination of Explicit PE:** This is a definite strength.  While the concept of NoPE has been explored in language models, its successful application to diffusion *transformers* for *image* generation, particularly for resolution extrapolation, is a novel contribution. The argument that explicit PEs limit resolution scaling because of extrapolation challenges is well-articulated.
*   **Implicit Positional Encoding via Causal Attention:** The use of causal attention for implicit position encoding is somewhat novel, even if the individual components are not entirely new. The paper's theoretical justification, linking token variance to position via causal attention, strengthens this point. The derivation showing the inverse relationship between variance and position is a solid contribution.
*   **Multi-Dilation Convolution:** The combination of causal attention with the multi-dilation convolution strategy addresses a key weakness, local detail, making the resulting approach more effective than either component independently.  The idea of using convolutions with varying receptive fields to avoid artifacts in higher resolution outputs is also novel.
*   **Ablation Study:** The component ablations are reasonably complete, convincingly illustrating the contributions of the causal attention and convolution modules.

**Significance:**

*   **Improved Resolution Extrapolation:** The paper's primary claim – that LEDiT significantly improves the ability of diffusion transformers to generate high-quality images at higher resolutions than their training data – is strongly supported by the experimental results.  The quantitative results consistently show LEDiT outperforming existing methods (DiT with NTK, YaRN, FiTv2).
*   **Practical Implications:**  The practical implications are significant. The ability to train models on lower-resolution datasets and then generate higher-resolution images without severe performance degradation can save substantial computational resources and improve the accessibility of high-quality image generation.
*   **Integration Potential:**  The demonstration that LEDiT can be effectively fine-tuned based on pre-trained DiT weights enhances its adoption potential. It suggests that LEDiT can be readily incorporated into existing text-to-image frameworks.
*   **Theoretical Justification:** The theoretical justification provided for causal attention is rigorous.
*   **Limitations:** The limitations section only briefly mentions the positional encoding, while there are others. The paper is focused heavily on the *technical* aspects of the architecture and evaluation. A more in-depth discussion about ethical considerations, potential biases amplified in higher-resolution generations, and negative social impacts would strengthen the work.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Comparison of Architectures:** The comparisons focus almost entirely on DiT variants and FiTv2. While these are appropriate baselines, including comparisons to other diffusion architectures or generative models that excel at resolution scaling could provide a broader context.
*   **Computational Cost:** While it mentions negligible cost convolution, the paper doesn't thoroughly compare the computational cost (training time, inference time, memory footprint) of LEDiT to other architectures, especially when generating high-resolution images. This would be valuable information for practitioners.
*   **Dependency on a Pre-trained VAE:** The method still relies on a pre-trained VAE (from Stable Diffusion). It would be better to know to what degree results depend on this VAE, for the extrapolation capabilities of the core LEDiT architecture.
*   **Theoretical Limitations**: the theory requires i.i.d conditions. While the paper discusses this, it could be more thoroughly addressed, perhaps with an alternative or approximation

**Justification for Score:**

I'm assigning a score of **7.5**. The paper presents a genuinely innovative architecture for length extrapolation in diffusion transformers. The elimination of explicit PEs and the use of causal attention for implicit encoding offer a compelling solution to a significant problem.  The empirical results strongly support the claims. The theoretical component adds depth to the paper, especially the variance derivation. However, the paper falls short of an exceptional score (8 or higher) due to the limited comparisons and the lack of deeper discussion of ethical considerations. The weaknesses outlined, particularly regarding the computational cost and dependency on the VAE also bring the score down slightly.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Large Language Models for Zero-shot Inference of Causal Structures in Biology
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04347v1)
- **Authors**: Izzy Newsham, Luka Kovačević, Richard Moulange, Nan Rosemary Ke, Sach Mukherjee
- **Abstract**: Genes, proteins and other biological entities influence one another via causal molecular networks. Causal relationships in such networks are mediated by complex and diverse mechanisms, through latent variables, and are often specific to cellular context. It remains challenging to characterise such networks in practice. Here, we present a novel framework to evaluate large language models (LLMs) for zero-shot inference of causal relationships in biology. In particular, we systematically evaluate causal claims obtained from an LLM using real-world interventional data. This is done over one hundred variables and thousands of causal hypotheses. Furthermore, we consider several prompting and retrieval-augmentation strategies, including large, and potentially conflicting, collections of scientific articles. Our results show that with tailored augmentation and prompting, even relatively small LLMs can capture meaningful aspects of causal structure in biological systems. This supports the notion that LLMs could act as orchestration tools in biological discovery, by helping to distil current knowledge in ways amenable to downstream analysis. Our approach to assessing LLMs with respect to experimental data is relevant for a broad range of problems at the intersection of causal learning, LLMs and scientific discovery.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the potential of large language models (LLMs) to infer causal relationships in biology in a zero-shot setting (without fine-tuning). The authors present a framework for evaluating LLMs on this task, using interventional data from Perturb-seq experiments to generate a causal ground truth. They evaluate various prompting and retrieval-augmentation strategies with the Gemma2 model, including specifying biological and experimental context, and compare the LLM's output to knowledge-driven baselines like the STRING database. The results suggest that even relatively small LLMs can capture meaningful aspects of causal structure with tailored augmentation and prompting, potentially serving as orchestration tools for biological discovery.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its systematic evaluation of LLMs for zero-shot causal inference in a complex biological setting using interventional experimental data. While LLMs have been used in biology for various tasks, a rigorous assessment of their ability to infer causal relationships from real-world data is a significant contribution. The exploration of different prompting strategies and their impact on performance is also valuable. However, other works have begun to evaluate CSL from LLM's, so this paper isn't breaking entirely new ground.
*   **Significance:** The potential significance of this work stems from the possibility of LLMs acting as "orchestration tools" for biological discovery. If LLMs can effectively distill knowledge from the literature and suggest potential causal relationships, they could significantly accelerate the research process by prioritizing experiments and generating hypotheses. The benchmark developed in the paper provides a valuable tool for assessing and improving LLM performance in this area. The implications for disease understanding and drug development are substantial.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Rigorous Evaluation:** The use of real-world interventional data from Perturb-seq experiments to generate a causal ground truth is a major strength. This provides a more realistic and relevant evaluation than using simulated data or curated knowledge bases alone.
    *   **Systematic Approach:** The authors systematically explore a range of prompting and retrieval-augmentation strategies, providing valuable insights into how to best leverage LLMs for this task.
    *   **Comparison to Baselines:** Comparing the LLM's performance to a knowledge-driven baseline (STRING) provides a useful benchmark and highlights the potential advantages of LLMs in this setting.
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and clearly presents the methodology, results, and conclusions.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Model Size:** The experiments are limited to the Gemma2-9B-it model due to computational constraints. Larger and more advanced LLMs may yield significantly better performance, and the current results may not fully reflect the potential of the approach.
    *   **Limited Scope:** The study focuses on a specific set of cancer-related genes and a particular experimental protocol. The generalizability of the findings to other biological systems and experimental settings is not fully established.
    *   **AUROC Scores:** The highest AUROC score of 0.625, while better than random chance, is not exceptionally high. This suggests that LLMs still have significant limitations in accurately inferring causal relationships in biology.
    *   **Lack of Direct Application:** The paper demonstrates the potential of LLMs for causal inference, but it does not directly apply this capability to solve a specific biological problem or make a novel discovery.
    *   **Prompt Sensitivity:** LLM performance is heavily influenced by prompt engineering and the specific context provided. The results may be sensitive to the choice of prompts, and further optimization may be needed to achieve optimal performance.
*   **Potential Impact:** The paper could potentially influence research in causal inference in biology and the application of LLMs to scientific discovery. The framework developed in the paper provides a valuable tool for assessing and improving LLM performance in this area. The results could also inspire further research into the use of LLMs for generating hypotheses, prioritizing experiments, and accelerating the research process. The code has not been released with this publication, which will limit the reproducibility and impact of the work until it is.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the novelty, significance, strengths, and weaknesses, a score of **7** is appropriate. The paper makes a solid contribution to the field by providing a systematic evaluation of LLMs for zero-shot causal inference in biology using real-world data. The approach is novel, and the benchmark developed in the paper is valuable. However, the limitations of the model size, scope, and AUROC scores, as well as the lack of a direct application, prevent the paper from receiving a higher score. While promising, the results indicate that LLMs are not yet capable of accurately inferring causal relationships in biology without further development and optimization.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Layer-Specific Scaling of Positional Encodings for Superior Long-Context Modeling
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04355v1)
- **Authors**: Zhenghua Wang, Yiran Ding, Changze Lv, Zhibo Xu, Tianlong Li, Tianyuan Shi, Xiaoqing Zheng, Xuanjing Huang
- **Abstract**: Although large language models (LLMs) have achieved significant progress in handling long-context inputs, they still suffer from the ``lost-in-the-middle'' problem, where crucial information in the middle of the context is often underrepresented or lost. Our extensive experiments reveal that this issue may arise from the rapid long-term decay in Rotary Position Embedding (RoPE). To address this problem, we propose a layer-specific positional encoding scaling method that assigns distinct scaling factors to each layer, slowing down the decay rate caused by RoPE to make the model pay more attention to the middle context. A specially designed genetic algorithm is employed to efficiently select the optimal scaling factors for each layer by incorporating Bezier curves to reduce the search space. Through comprehensive experimentation, we demonstrate that our method significantly alleviates the ``lost-in-the-middle'' problem. Our approach results in an average accuracy improvement of up to 20% on the Key-Value Retrieval dataset. Furthermore, we show that layer-specific interpolation, as opposed to uniform interpolation across all layers, enhances the model's extrapolation capabilities when combined with PI and Dynamic-NTK positional encoding schemes.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the "lost-in-the-middle" problem in large language models (LLMs), where the model struggles to effectively process information located in the middle of long contexts. The authors hypothesize that this is due to the rapid long-term decay of Rotary Positional Embeddings (RoPE). To mitigate this, they propose a layer-specific positional encoding scaling method, assigning distinct scaling factors to each layer to slow down the decay and allow the model to pay more attention to the middle context. They use a genetic algorithm, constrained by Bézier curves, to efficiently search for the optimal scaling factors for each layer.  The method shows significant accuracy improvements, particularly on the Key-Value Retrieval dataset, and demonstrates improved extrapolation capabilities when combined with other positional encoding schemes.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of layer-specific scaling of positional encodings is reasonably novel. While other approaches have explored scaling attention or positional embeddings, the paper's focus on *layer-specific* scaling, driven by an understanding of how different layers process information in a long context, is a unique contribution. The use of Bézier curves and a genetic algorithm to efficiently search the scaling factor space is also a strong methodological contribution.

*   **Significance:** The "lost-in-the-middle" problem is a significant bottleneck in LLM performance for long-context tasks.  The demonstrated improvements in accuracy and extrapolation ability, particularly on retrieval tasks, indicate that this approach has practical value.  The fact that the method maintains inference speed (compared to other approaches that dynamically adjust attention during inference) adds to its significance. However, the experiments are performed primarily on 7B models. The paper does provide a solid base and could have a substantial impact if the findings are validated and extended to larger models.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper is well-motivated by the "lost-in-the-middle" problem and provides a clear hypothesis about RoPE decay.
    *   The layer-specific approach makes intuitive sense, given the varying roles of different layers in LLMs.
    *   The combination of Bézier curves and a genetic algorithm offers an efficient way to search the scaling factor space.
    *   The experiments demonstrate significant performance improvements on several datasets, particularly on Key-Value Retrieval.
    *   The inference speed efficiency is a major advantage.
    *   Extensive experiments on different contexts with a solid analytical component explaining the reasoning behind design choices.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The experiments are mostly limited to 7B models. The effectiveness on larger, more complex models is not fully established. This limitation weakens the claims about the broad applicability of the method. The ability to scale up and show similar improvements with larger models will significantly increase its importance.
    *   The lack of a detailed hyperparameter search process can be seen as a limitation. A more thorough hyperparameter study may reveal better performance.
    *   The analysis and justification for the specific shape and parameters of the Beziér curves could be more deeply explored to provide more insight and reproducibility.
    *   The paper could benefit from a more comprehensive comparative analysis against other related methods that attempt to address the "lost-in-the-middle" issue. Although existing methods are mentioned, a quantitative comparison would strengthen the paper.
    * The limitation section mentions not exploring models that have tricks to address the lost-in-the-middle issue, but it may have been informative to study how well the methods perform on top of those tricks.

*   **Potential Influence:**  If the findings generalize to larger models, this method could become a standard technique for improving long-context LLMs. It provides a relatively simple and efficient way to address a critical problem. It also opens up new research directions focused on understanding the specific roles of different layers in long-context processing.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper demonstrates a novel and effective approach to addressing a significant problem in LLMs. The layer-specific scaling method, combined with an efficient search algorithm, shows promise in improving performance for long-context tasks without sacrificing inference speed. However, the primary limitation is the lack of extensive validation on larger models. More evidence across a wide range of LLM sizes would increase the paper's impact significantly. While the improvement in speed over other dynamic methods is a strong selling point, further studies should be done to understand how it scales across different batch sizes or other hardware.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Lost in Literalism: How Supervised Training Shapes Translationese in LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04369v1)
- **Authors**: Yafu Li, Ronghao Zhang, Zhilin Wang, Huajian Zhang, Leyang Cui, Yongjing Yin, Tong Xiao, Yue Zhang
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have achieved remarkable success in machine translation, demonstrating impressive performance across diverse languages. However, translationese, characterized by overly literal and unnatural translations, remains a persistent challenge in LLM-based translation systems. Despite their pre-training on vast corpora of natural utterances, LLMs exhibit translationese errors and generate unexpected unnatural translations, stemming from biases introduced during supervised fine-tuning (SFT). In this work, we systematically evaluate the prevalence of translationese in LLM-generated translations and investigate its roots during supervised training. We introduce methods to mitigate these biases, including polishing golden references and filtering unnatural training instances. Empirical evaluations demonstrate that these approaches significantly reduce translationese while improving translation naturalness, validated by human evaluations and automatic metrics. Our findings highlight the need for training-aware adjustments to optimize LLM translation outputs, paving the way for more fluent and target-language-consistent translations. We release the data and code at https://github.com/yafuly/LLM_Translationese.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Lost in Literalism: How Supervised Training Shapes Translationese in LLMs" investigates the prevalence and causes of "translationese" (overly literal and unnatural translations) in large language model (LLM)-based machine translation systems. It argues that despite pre-training on vast corpora of natural language, LLMs exhibit translationese due to biases introduced during supervised fine-tuning (SFT). The authors systematically evaluate translationese in LLM-generated translations, using human annotation to identify specific error types and measure their frequency. They propose and test mitigation strategies: polishing golden references in SFT data and filtering unnatural training instances. Empirical results show that these methods significantly reduce translationese and improve translation naturalness.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Novelty:** The paper addresses a relevant and under-explored aspect of LLM-based translation. While previous research has focused on adequacy and fluency, the systematic investigation of translationese in LLMs is a valuable contribution. It fills a gap in the literature by explicitly examining how SFT can negatively impact naturalness despite the availability of natural language data during pre-training.
    *   **Rigorous Methodology:** The paper employs a well-defined methodology, including human annotation by expert translators to identify and categorize translationese errors. The use of a quantitative metric (Translationese Span Ratio - TSR) provides an objective measure of translationese prevalence. The experimental setup with different LLMs and language pairs enhances the generalizability of the findings.
    *   **Practical Mitigation Strategies:** The proposed mitigation strategies, such as polishing golden references and filtering unnatural training instances, are practical and easy to implement. Their effectiveness is demonstrated through empirical evaluations, including both automatic metrics and human evaluations.
    *   **Reproducibility:** The authors provide a clear description of the experimental setup, training details, and evaluation metrics, increasing the reproducibility of the study. The release of data and code further promotes research in this area.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope of Language Pairs:** The primary focus on English-Chinese and German-English translations, while comprehensive, limits the generalizability of the findings to other language pairs, especially low-resource or morphologically rich languages. The paper acknowledges this limitation.
    *   **Subjectivity in Human Evaluation:** Human evaluation, although conducted by experts, inherently involves a degree of subjectivity. While inter-annotator agreement is reported, the potential for bias in identifying translationese errors remains.
    *   **Limited Exploration of Alternative Mitigation Techniques:** While polishing and filtering show promise, the paper could benefit from exploring alternative mitigation techniques, such as incorporating explicit naturalness constraints during training or using adversarial training to discriminate between natural and translationese outputs.
    *   **Incremental improvements:** The improvements shown in the paper are consistent but limited, it is unclear if the approaches would scale well across a wider range of translation pairs.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper provides valuable insights into the challenges of achieving naturalness in LLM-based translation and highlights the importance of training-aware adjustments.
    *   The proposed mitigation strategies offer a practical approach to reduce translationese and improve translation quality.
    *   The findings contribute to a better understanding of how SFT influences LLM behavior and can inform the design of more effective training methods.
    *   The quantitative analysis of translationese using the TSR metric provides a useful tool for evaluating and comparing different translation systems.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper represents a solid and useful contribution to the field of machine translation, specifically in the context of LLMs. While the scope is somewhat limited by the language pairs considered and the incremental nature of the improvements, the rigorous methodology, practical mitigation strategies, and insightful findings justify a high score. The paper provides valuable insights and actionable recommendations for addressing a relevant and under-explored aspect of LLM-based translation.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### TRACT: Regression-Aware Fine-tuning Meets Chain-of-Thought Reasoning for LLM-as-a-Judge
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04381v1)
- **Authors**: Cheng-Han Chiang, Hung-yi Lee, Michal Lukasik
- **Abstract**: The LLM-as-a-judge paradigm uses large language models (LLMs) for automated text evaluation, where a numerical assessment is assigned by an LLM to the input text following scoring rubrics. Existing methods for LLM-as-a-judge use cross-entropy (CE) loss for fine-tuning, which neglects the numeric nature of score prediction. Recent work addresses numerical prediction limitations of LLM fine-tuning through regression-aware fine-tuning, which, however, does not consider chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning for score prediction. In this paper, we introduce TRACT (Two-stage Regression-Aware fine-tuning with CoT), a method combining CoT reasoning with regression-aware training. TRACT consists of two stages: first, seed LLM is fine-tuned to generate CoTs, which serve as supervision for the second stage fine-tuning. The training objective of TRACT combines the CE loss for learning the CoT reasoning capabilities, and the regression-aware loss for the score prediction. Experiments across four LLM-as-a-judge datasets and two LLMs show that TRACT significantly outperforms existing methods. Extensive ablation studies validate the importance of each component in TRACT.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "TRACT: Regression-Aware Fine-tuning Meets Chain-of-Thought Reasoning for LLM-as-a-Judge":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces TRACT (Two-stage Regression-Aware fine-tuning with CoT), a novel fine-tuning method designed to enhance the performance of Large Language Models (LLMs) used in the "LLM-as-a-judge" paradigm. This paradigm involves using LLMs to automatically assess text quality based on predefined rubrics.  TRACT addresses a limitation in existing methods, which typically use cross-entropy (CE) loss for fine-tuning. CE loss is suboptimal for numerical score prediction as it doesn't account for the numeric nature of scores.  While regression-aware fine-tuning methods exist (e.g., RAFT), they often lack Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning. TRACT combines both.

The method consists of two stages:

1.  **CoT Generation:** The seed LLM is first fine-tuned to generate CoTs (reasoning traces) which are then used as supervision.
2.  **Regression-Aware Fine-tuning:**  The model is further fine-tuned using a combined objective: CE loss to learn CoT reasoning and regression-aware loss (RAFT loss) to improve score prediction.

Experiments on four datasets and using two LLMs (Mistral-7B-Instruct-v0.2 and Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct) demonstrate that TRACT significantly outperforms various baselines, including Prometheus-2-7B. Ablation studies validate the importance of each component within TRACT. The released models achieve state-of-the-art performance for their size, especially in scenarios with limited inference-time compute.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The key novelty lies in the effective integration of CoT reasoning with regression-aware fine-tuning for the specific task of LLM-as-a-judge. While both CoT and regression-aware methods have been explored separately, the specific combination and the two-stage training process are innovative. The use of self-generated CoTs in the second stage to align the training and inference distributions of CoTs is a notable contribution.  The negative results obtained by applying CE to self-generated data (highlighting the effectiveness of RAFT in this context) is also of value.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance is multi-faceted:

    *   **Improved LLM-as-a-Judge:** The results demonstrate a considerable improvement in the performance of LLMs acting as judges, which has implications for automated evaluation, especially within the realm of LLM development itself.
    *   **Addressing a Specific Limitation:** TRACT directly tackles the limitations of standard CE loss for numerical prediction tasks.
    *   **Practical Utility:**  The models are open-sourced, facilitating wider adoption and further research.  The model performs well even under limited computational budgets.
    *   **Insightful Ablation Studies:**  The ablation studies provide valuable insights into the importance of each component of the TRACT framework (self-generated CoTs, the two-stage training, CoT-RAFT loss) which inform future research.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The problem is well-motivated and clearly defined.
    *   The method is technically sound and well-explained.
    *   The experiments are comprehensive, including multiple datasets, models, and baselines.
    *   The ablation studies are thorough and provide valuable insights.
    *   The writing is clear and concise.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Reliance on GPT-4 for Training Data:** The initial training data still relies on GPT-4, which might introduce biases or limitations inherited from that model. While self-generated data is used, it builds on this initial GPT-4 data.
    *   **Limited Scope:** While the datasets used represent a variety of LLM-as-a-judge use cases, the evaluation is still focused on text evaluation tasks.  It is unclear how well the method will generalize to other tasks with numerical outputs or whether different amounts of CoT sampling will be needed to optimize performance.
    *   **Minor Lack of Clarity:** There were cases where the exact implementations had to be inferred, a small annoyance which might hinder reproducibility.

*   **Potential Influence:** TRACT has the potential to influence future research in LLM fine-tuning for tasks involving numerical output and subjective evaluations. It suggests a promising direction for combining CoT reasoning with regression-aware training. It is also likely to be adopted in practical applications of LLM-as-a-judge for automated evaluation.

**Justification for Score:**

I assign a score of **8**. The paper presents a novel and effective method (TRACT) to address a specific limitation in the LLM-as-a-judge paradigm. The experimental results are convincing, the ablation studies are insightful, and the models are released to the public. It builds well upon existing research, especially RAFT, providing a meaningful step forward for the field. The weaknesses are minor and do not detract significantly from the overall contribution. The reliance on GPT-4 for the training data is perhaps the most significant limitation, but the use of self-generated data in the second stage mitigates this somewhat.  The paper is likely to be widely cited and influence future research in this area.
Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Shaping Shared Languages: Human and Large Language Models' Inductive Biases in Emergent Communication
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04395v1)
- **Authors**: Tom Kouwenhoven, Max Peeperkorn, Roy de Kleijn, Tessa Verhoef
- **Abstract**: Languages are shaped by the inductive biases of their users. Using a classical referential game, we investigate how artificial languages evolve when optimised for inductive biases in humans and large language models (LLMs) via Human-Human, LLM-LLM and Human-LLM experiments. We show that referentially grounded vocabularies emerge that enable reliable communication in all conditions, even when humans and LLMs collaborate. Comparisons between conditions reveal that languages optimised for LLMs subtly differ from those optimised for humans. Interestingly, interactions between humans and LLMs alleviate these differences and result in vocabularies which are more human-like than LLM-like. These findings advance our understanding of how inductive biases in LLMs play a role in the dynamic nature of human language and contribute to maintaining alignment in human and machine communication. In particular, our work underscores the need to think of new methods that include human interaction in the training processes of LLMs, and shows that using communicative success as a reward signal can be a fruitful, novel direction.
- **Summary**: Okay, I can provide a summary and critical evaluation of the paper based on the provided text.

**Summary:**

This paper investigates how human and large language models (LLMs) can collaboratively shape artificial languages through a referential game framework. The study explores how the inductive biases of both humans and LLMs influence the evolution of these languages in Human-Human, LLM-LLM, and Human-LLM interaction scenarios. The results demonstrate that referentially grounded vocabularies emerge in all conditions, enabling successful communication. Languages optimized for LLMs exhibit subtle differences compared to those optimized for humans.  Notably, Human-LLM interactions lead to vocabularies that are more human-like than LLM-like. The authors argue that these findings contribute to understanding the role of LLM inductive biases in language dynamics and suggest that incorporating human interaction in LLM training processes is essential for maintaining alignment and promoting successful communication.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its direct comparison of language evolution across three interaction settings (Human-Human, LLM-LLM, and Human-LLM) within a single experimental framework.  While prior work has explored language evolution in human and LLM settings separately (Kouwenhoven et al., 2025), the direct comparison, particularly the Human-LLM collaborative scenario, provides a novel perspective on how these distinct learning systems influence each other.

*   **Significance:** The findings have implications for the broader field of human-computer interaction (HCI) and LLM development. The demonstration that Human-LLM collaborations can lead to more human-like languages suggests a promising direction for improving alignment and communication between humans and AI.  The paper also underscores the importance of considering and mitigating LLM inductive biases to prevent undesirable language drift or the emergence of non-human-interpretable language models, which is very relevant.  The emphasis on communicative success as a reward signal offers a potential alternative to solely relying on large datasets of human-generated text for training LLMs. This could have practical benefits for reducing model bias.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The experimental design is well-structured and builds upon established language evolution paradigms (Lewis game, iterated learning).
    *   The use of quantitative metrics (TopSim, Ngram diversity, etc.) allows for a rigorous comparison of language structure across different conditions.
    *   The discussion is well-grounded in relevant literature, drawing upon both human language evolution research and recent advancements in LLM understanding.
    *   The study is reproducible, with a clear description of the methodology and prompt.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The paper acknowledges that the results are methodologically dependent. The choice of the tokeniser, use of JSON format, and prompting method are influential.
    *   The specific LLM used (Llama 3 70B) and the prompt engineering (e.g. prompting for communicative success) may limit the generalizability of the findings to other models or settings. Exploring this with smaller models revealed that it was less successful. This indicates that certain findings may not be universally applicable.
    *   A deeper dive into *why* LLMs exhibit certain biases or how those biases interact with human preferences would have further strengthened the analysis.  The authors mention the need to examine communicative success as a signal, however a more detailed inspection would further strengthen the conclusions.
    *   The study primarily focuses on a simplified communication task using artificial languages.  The extent to which these findings translate to more complex, natural language scenarios needs to be further investigated.

**Justification of Score:**

Given the novelty of the direct comparison between Human-Human, LLM-LLM, and Human-LLM language evolution, the practical implications for LLM alignment and human-AI communication, and a well-executed experimental design, the paper warrants a high score. However, the potential for methodological dependencies, the limitations of the artificial language setting, and the lack of deeper mechanistic explanations prevent it from achieving a perfect score.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### TableLoRA: Low-rank Adaptation on Table Structure Understanding for Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04396v1)
- **Authors**: Xinyi He, Yihao Liu, Mengyu Zhou, Yeye He, Haoyu Dong, Shi Han, Zejian Yuan, Dongmei Zhang
- **Abstract**: Tabular data are crucial in many fields and their understanding by large language models (LLMs) under high parameter efficiency paradigm is important. However, directly applying parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) techniques to tabular tasks presents significant challenges, particularly in terms of better table serialization and the representation of two-dimensional structured information within a one-dimensional sequence. To address this, we propose TableLoRA, a module designed to improve LLMs' understanding of table structure during PEFT. It incorporates special tokens for serializing tables with special token encoder and uses 2D LoRA to encode low-rank information on cell positions. Experiments on four tabular-related datasets demonstrate that TableLoRA consistently outperforms vanilla LoRA and surpasses various table encoding methods tested in control experiments. These findings reveal that TableLoRA, as a table-specific LoRA, enhances the ability of LLMs to process tabular data effectively, especially in low-parameter settings, demonstrating its potential as a robust solution for handling table-related tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "TableLoRA: Low-rank Adaptation on Table Structure Understanding for Large Language Models":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces TableLoRA, a novel module designed to improve the ability of Large Language Models (LLMs) to understand and process tabular data during Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning (PEFT).
